- Cell Structure - Overview: - Studied in cell biology; electron microscope (1940) enabled detailed observation of complex cell structures. - Basic Components: - Outer layering: - Cell membrane (plasma membrane) or cell wall. - Nucleus. - Cytoplasm (protoplasm). - Cell organelles. - Interactions: - Components interact with the environment to perform various functions. - Cell Wall - Definition: - Rigid, non-living, flexible covering outside the cell membrane; provides shape, structural support, and protection from mechanical injury and infection. - Presence: - Found in plants, fungi, bacteria, algae, some archaebacteria; absent in animals and protozoans. - Composition: - Bacterial: - Peptidoglycan. - Fungal: - Chitin (glucosamine polymer). - Algal: - Glycoproteins, polysaccharides. - Plant: - Cellulose (provides structural strength). - Structure: -
LayerDescription
Outermost Layer (Middle Lamella)Rich in pectins, glues adjacent plant cells together
Primary Cell WallThin, flexible, single-layered; formed during cell growth; made of cellulose, hemicellulose, polysaccharides
Secondary Cell WallThick, 2–3 layers; formed when cell is fully grown; contains lignin; not in all cells
- Functions: - Acts as a pressure vessel, preventing bursting in plants, fungi, and bacteria. - Tolerates greater environmental changes compared to animal cells. - Cell Membrane (Plasma Membrane) - Definition: - Outermost covering separating cell contents from the external environment; termed by C. Nageli and C. Grammer (1885) as ‘cell membrane,’ Pffeffer as ‘plasma membrane.’ - Composition: - Lipids (primarily phospholipids), proteins; thin, flexible, semi-permeable/selectively permeable. - Presence: - Found in animal, plant, prokaryotic, and fungal cells; absent in viruses. - Functions: - Maintains cell shape and support. - Selectively permits movement of ions and organic molecules. - Enables endocytosis (e.g., in Amoeba) for engulfing food/substances. - Serves as an attachment base for cytoskeleton (some organisms) or cell wall. - Facilitates cell adhesion, ion conductivity, and cell signaling. - Nucleus - Discovery: - Robert Brown (1831). - Description: - Spherical or elliptical, centrally located; the cell’s control center (‘brain’). - Components: - Nuclear Membrane (Nuclear Envelope, Karyotheca): - Double-layered (protein, fat); encloses nucleus contents. - Perinuclear space between layers; contains nuclear pores for material transfer. - Outer layer connects to endoplasmic reticulum. - Nucleoplasm (Karyoplasm, Nuclear Sap): - Acidic protoplasm inside nucleus; contains nucleolus, chromatin. - Composed of proteins, phosphorus, nucleic acids. - Nucleolus: - Discovered by Fontana (1874); spherical, non-membrane-bound structures in nucleoplasm. - Typically 1–4 per nucleus; synthesizes rRNA, stores genetic information in DNA. - Nuclear Matrix: - Network of acid protein fibrils; scaffolds chromatin. - Chromatin/Chromosomes: - Chromatin: - DNA-protein (histone) complex, stains with dyes; responsible for genetic traits. - Chromosomes: - Condensed chromatin during cell division; thread-like, rod-shaped; composed of matrix, two chromatids (chromonemata). - Genes on chromosomes control trait inheritance (‘hereditary vehicles’). - Cytoplasm/Protoplasm - Discovery: - Dujardin (1835); termed ‘protoplasm’ by Purkinje (1839). - Definition: - Viscous, fluid-like substance filling the cell; living content surrounded by plasma membrane. - Composition: - Nucleic acids, proteins, lipids, polysaccharides. - Eukaryotes: - Cytoplasm (between cell membrane and nuclear envelope); nucleoplasm (inside nucleus). - Prokaryotes: - Bacterial cytoplasm (inside plasma membrane). - Cell Organelles - Definition: - Specialized components within cells performing specific functions (e.g., synthesis, waste cleanup). - Types and Functions: - Endoplasmic Reticulum (ER): - Discovery: - Albert Claude, Keith Porter (1945). - Structure: - Network of tubules or flattened sacs (vesicles); interconnected by a continuous membrane. - Types: -
TypeCharacteristics
Rough ER (RER)Ribosomes on cytoplasmic side; synthesizes/secretes serum proteins (e.g., albumin), hormones (e.g., insulin), milk in glands
Smooth ER (SER)Smooth surface; synthesizes lipids/steroids, metabolizes carbohydrates, regulates calcium, detoxifies drugs, attaches receptors
- Functions: - SER: - Lipid synthesis for membrane biogenesis. - Material transport within the cell. - Waste disposal and cell cleansing via digestive enzymes in membrane-bound sacs. - Breaks down worn-out cellular components. - Golgi Apparatus (Golgi Body): - Discovery: - Camillo Golgi (1898) in nerve cells of barn owl and cat. - Structure: - Flat sacs (cisternae), stacked in a bent/semicircular shape; includes tubules, vesicles; linked to ER membrane. - Functions: - Processes proteins from ER. - Transports lipids within cells. - Forms complex sugars from simple sugars. - Contributes to cell wall (plants) and lysosome formation. - Lysosomes: - Discovery: - C. De Duve (1955). - Nicknames: - Suicidal bags, digestive bags, atom bomb of the cell (due to hydrolytic enzymes). - Structure: - Complex lumen in packets; found in animal cells, protozoans. - Functions: - Intracellular (via phagocytosis) and extracellular (via exocytosis) digestion. - Disposes of aging/dead cells, replacing with new ones. - Supports cell defense system. - Mitochondria: - Discovery: - AV Kolliker (1850, plants), Richard Altman (1880, animals); named by C. Benda. - Nickname: - Powerhouse or power plant of the cell. - Structure: - Elliptical, double-membraned (outer: porous, smooth; inner: folded into cristae/tubuli). - Characteristics: - Contains own DNA, ribosomes; semi-autonomous (partially controlled by nucleus, depends on cytoplasm). - Functions: - Converts energy into ATP for cellular activities (e.g., muscle contraction, nerve impulse conduction). - Stores/releases calcium as needed. - Involved in cell division, growth, and apoptosis. - Plastids: - Discovery: - E. Hackel (1866); termed by Schimper (1883). - Description: - Found in plant and algal cells; multiple membrane layers in stroma; contain DNA, ribosomes. - Types: -
TypeCharacteristicsExamples
LeucoplastColorless; in non-green cells (e.g., roots); stores starch (amyloplast), fat (elaioplast), proteins (proteinoplast)-
ChromoplastYellow/red due to carotenoids; in colored parts (e.g., flowers, fruits)Lycopene (tomato), carotene (carrot), betamin (beetroot)
ChloroplastGreen due to chlorophyll, carotenoids; performs photosynthesis; non-green algal chloroplasts called chromatophores-
- Note: - Chloroplast term by Ajw Schimper (1883). - Function: - Manufactures/stores chemical compounds. - Ribosomes: - Discovery: - George Palade (1953); initially called microsomes. - Structure: - Granular, composed of RNA and proteins; no membrane; 80S in eukaryotes, 70S in prokaryotes. - Function: - Protein synthesis (‘factory of protein’). - Centrosomes: - Discovery: - T. Boveri (1888). - Structure: - Contains two perpendicular centrioles (cartwheel-like). - Presence: - Mainly in animal cells. - Functions: - Assists in cell division and spindle fiber formation. - Forms cilia and flagella. - Vacuoles: - Description: - Membrane-bound organelles in plants, fungi, some protists, animals, bacteria; filled with water, molecules, enzymes; variable shape/size. - Formation: - Fusion of membrane vesicles. - Types: - Sap Vacuoles: - Fluid-filled, separated by tonoplast; maintain osmotic/turgor pressure; large in plants, small/few in animals. - Gas Vacuoles: - Store gases for buoyancy, strength, radiation protection; found in green bacterial cells. - Functions: - Stores foodstuffs (sugars, \( \text{O}_2 \), \( \text{CO}_2 \), minerals, organic acids, residuals). - Maintains cell turgidity and transports cellular substances. - In unicellular organisms, removes excess water/waste. - Microbodies: - Description: - Small, single-membrane-bound organelles; absorb oxygen, perform oxidation (non-respiratory). - Types: - Peroxisomes: - Developed from ER; contain peroxins for material uptake; discovered by Tolbert and Yamazaki (1969) in liver cells. - Glyoxysomes: - Plant-specific peroxisomes; contain enzymes for β-oxidation of fatty acids and glyoxylate pathway. - Cell Cycle and Cell Division - Overview - All organisms begin from a single cell, growing by adding new cells through division of pre-existing cells. - Rudolf Virchow (1858): - Proposed *Omnis Cellula e Cellula* (‘every cell comes from a cell’), emphasizing that life continuity depends on cell division. - Cell Cycle - - Definition: - Introduced by Howard and Pele (1953); a series of events where a cell duplicates its genome, synthesizes components, and divides into two daughter cells. - Phases: - Interphase (Undividing Phase) - M Phase (Mitosis Phase, Dividing Phase) - Phases of Cell Cycle - Interphase (Undividing Phase): - Description: - Occurs between two M phases; lasts >95% of the cell cycle; not a resting phase but a preparation for division through growth and DNA replication. - Subphases: -
PhaseDescription
G1 Phase (First Gap)Time between mitosis and DNA replication; cells are metabolically active, grow continuously, produce carbohydrates, lipids, proteins; DNA content remains constant (2C)
S Phase (Synthesis)DNA replication occurs; DNA content doubles from 2C to 4C
G2 Phase (Second Gap)Preparatory phase for division; synthesizes proteins, RNA, leading to mitosis
- Note: - Some cells enter a quiescent G0 phase (resting state) instead of proceeding to division. - M Phase (Dividing Phase): - Description: - Involves division of duplicated chromosomes into two daughter nuclei (karyokinesis) followed by cytoplasm division (cytokinesis). - Characteristics: - Chromosomes remain the same in parent and daughter cells (equational division). - Cell Division - Overview: - Essential for all living organisms; first proposed by Nageli, observed by Flemming (1882) in reptilian *Triturus masculus*, named ‘mitosis’; extensively studied by Belar (1920). - Modes of Cell Division: - Amitosis - Mitosis - Meiosis - Amitosis - - Description: - Rare, not a precise division mode; occurs in specialized cells (e.g., mammalian cartilage, embryonic membranes, old/diseased tissues). - Process: - Direct division of nucleus and cytoplasm without structured phases, producing two daughter cells. - Mitosis - Overview: - Explained by Eduard Strasburger; occurs in somatic cells (e.g., skin, liver, bone) and gonads for germ cell multiplication; produces two identical cells with the same chromosome number (equational division). - Phases of Mitosis: -
PhaseDurationDescription
Prophase~50 minutesLongest phase; chromosomal material condenses; nucleoli, nuclear envelope disappear; duplicated chromosomes (two sister chromatids) held by kinetochore at centromere; spindles push centrioles to opposite poles (no centrioles in plants)
Metaphase2–10 minutesShort phase; chromosomes align at equatorial plane (metaphase plate); spindle fibers attach to kinetochore; ideal for studying chromosome number/morphology
Anaphase2–3 minutesCentromeres split; sister chromatids separate, move to opposite poles; spindle fibers elongate, cell lengthens; ends when chromatids reach poles
Telophase-Chromosomes decondense, lose individuality; nuclear envelope reconstructs; chromosomes uncoil, become slender; spindle fibers disappear; cytoplasm viscosity decreases; organelles (nucleolus, Golgi, ER) reform
Cytokinesis-Divides cytoplasm, organelles equally into two daughter cells; in animals, via cleavage furrow (contracting microfilaments); in plants, via cell plate formation
- Significance: - Facilitates growth, development in multicellular organisms. - Aids in wound healing, tissue repair. - Maintains chromosome number, nucleocytoplasmic ratio. - Meiosis - Overview: - Term coined by JB Farmer and Moore (1905); restricted to reproductive cells, producing gametes (sex cells) or asexual reproductive bodies (spores); reduces chromosome number by half (reduction division). - Occurrence: - In animals, some lower plants, protists, fungi. - Stages: - Meiosis I (Heterotypic Division): - Reduces chromosome number from diploid (2n) to haploid (n). - Phases: -
PhaseDescription
Prophase ILongest phase (>90% of meiosis time); divided into 5 sub-stages: leptotene, zygotene, pachytene, diplotene, diakinesis; involves chromosome condensation, pairing
Metaphase ISpindle shifts to nucleus position; bivalent chromosomes (synapsed pairs) align at equator, attached by centromeres to spindle fibers
Anaphase IHomologous chromosomes separate; half move to each pole; each chromosome retains two chromatids
Telophase IChromosomes reach poles, uncoil, elongate; cytoplasm divides via cleavage (animals) or cell plate (plants); forms two cells with one nucleus each
- Meiosis II: - Similar to mitosis; no interphase before prophase II; haploid nuclei divide mitotically to produce four haploid daughter nuclei. - Phases: - Prophase II, Metaphase II, Anaphase II, Telophase II. - Outcome: - One diploid nucleus produces four haploid nuclei. - Significance: - Maintains chromosome number stability across generations. - Increases genetic variability through recombination, aiding evolution. - Produces gametes/spores for reproduction. - Tissues - Definition: - A group of structurally similar cells that work together to perform a specific function, as coined by N. Grew. - Examples: - Blood, phloem, muscle. - Study of Tissues: - The branch of biology dealing with tissues is called histology. - Broad Categories: - Plant Tissues - Animal Tissues - Plant Tissues - - Overview: - Most plant tissues are dead, providing greater mechanical strength compared to living cells, requiring less maintenance. - Classification Based on Function: - Meristematic Tissue - Permanent Tissue - Types of Plant Tissues: - Meristematic Tissue: - Definition: - Comprises cells that remain in a continuous state of division or retain the ability to divide actively throughout their life. - These cells differentiate to form permanent tissues. - Locations: - Found in growing parts such as the receptacle axis of the stem, sepal, stigma, root apex, and cambium. - Characteristics: - Cells are rounded, oval, polygonal, or rectangular with no intercellular spaces. - Living cells contribute to increased length and girth of the plant. - Thin cell walls made of cellulose, lacking vacuoles. - Contain dense protoplasm and large nuclei. - Plastids present as protoplastids. - Types Based on Position: - Apical Meristems: - Location: - Found at the apices (growing tips) of main and lateral shoots and roots. - Function: - Responsible for linear growth (primary growth), increasing height or length. - Intercalary Meristems: - Location: - Found at the base of leaves or internodes on twigs, e.g., stems of grasses, wheat, base of pine leaves, or mint nodes. - Function: - Contribute to longitudinal growth by adding primary tissue. - Lateral Meristems (Cambium): - Location: - Found on lateral sides of stems and roots, e.g., cork cambium, vascular cambium. - Function: - Divide in one plane, increasing diameter (secondary growth) in dicotyledons, absent in monocotyledons. - Additional Notes: - Secondary growth forms annual rings, used to estimate tree age. - Cambium activity varies with seasons: high in spring, low in winter, indicating one year’s growth. - Permanent Tissue: - Definition: - Composed of mature cells that have lost the ability to divide, having undergone differentiation to adopt specific shapes, sizes, and functions. - Originate from meristematic tissues. - May be living or dead, with thick or thin cell walls. - Types: - Simple Permanent Tissue: - Definition: - Composed of similar cell types with common origin and function. - Subtypes: - Parenchyma: - Characteristics: - Most simple and unspecialized, with isodiametric cells (equally expanded) and intercellular spaces for gas exchange. - Living cells, oval, round, polygonal, or elongated, with cellulose or calcium pectate cell walls. - Each cell has a nucleus and a large vacuole. - Locations: - Found in epidermis, cortex, pith, pericycle, mesophyll of leaves, fruit pulp, seed endoderm, and near meristematic tissues. - Functions: - When containing chloroplasts, called chlorenchyma, performs photosynthesis. - Gives rise to secondary meristems (cork cambium, vascular cambium). - Stores food in protoplasm. - Collenchyma: - Characteristics: - Living cells, elongated with irregular thickening of cellulose and pectin in cell walls, no intercellular spaces. - Functions: - Provides mechanical support and elasticity, offering rigid support. - Locations: - Outer cortex of dicot stems, angles of stems, midribs of leaves. - Sclerenchyma: - Characteristics: - Dead cells, devoid of protoplasm, with thickened cell walls due to lignin or cellulose. - Functions: - Provides mechanical strength, support, and rigidity; forms hard coatings of seeds and nuts (e.g., coconut husk). - Complex Permanent Tissue: - Definition: - Composed of multiple cell types with common origin. - Subtypes: - Xylem: - Function: - Chief conducting tissue for water and inorganic solutes (minerals) from roots to apical parts. - Provides mechanical strength and rigidity. - Components: - Tracheids, vessels, xylem parenchyma, xylem fibers. - Vessels are the most important cells. - Characteristics: - Consists of dead hollow cells (e.g., wood) in the form of fibers and sclereids. - Locations: - Found in vascular pores of roots, stems, and leaves. - Phloem: - Function: - Chief conducting tissue for organic solutes (food), with bidirectional conduction (leaves to storage organs or storage to growing parts). - Components: - Sieve tubes, companion cells, phloem parenchyma, phloem fibers (dead cells). - Sieve tubes have porous walls for food distribution. - Additional Notes: - Also called bast, as phloem fibers are used for binding (e.g., flax, hemp). - Additional Tissue Types: - Vascular Tissues: - Comprise xylem and phloem, forming vascular bundles. - Laticiferous Tissues: - Contain colorless, milky fluid called latex. - Secretory Tissues: - Secrete substances like rubber, resins, oils, gums; found in vascular plants (e.g., hydathodes, trichomes, mucus glands, secretory cavities/canals). - Animal Tissues - Overview: - Organized layers or masses of structurally similar cells with common embryonic origin and function. - Four basic types: epithelial, muscular, connective, neural (nervous). - Types and Functions: -
NameOriginMain Function
Epithelial TissueEctoderm, Mesoderm, EndodermProtection, secretion, absorption, excretion, reproduction
Connective TissueMesodermAttachment, support, protection, storage, transport
Muscular TissueMesodermMovement of parts and locomotion
Neural TissueEctodermControl and coordination by nerve impulse conduction
- Epithelial Tissue (Epithelia): - Definition: - Comprises one or more layers of closely packed cells covering external surfaces and internal linings of body organs and ducts. - Forms barriers to separate body systems. - Originates from ectoderm, mesoderm, or endoderm (e.g., skin, mouth lining, blood vessels, lung alveoli, kidney tubules). - Characteristics: - Tightly packed cells with minimal cementing material, forming a continuous sheet with no intercellular spaces. - Rests on a non-cellular basement membrane, separating it from connective tissue. - Protects against microorganisms, injury, and fluid loss. - Outer layer exposed to the environment; inner layer acts as basement membrane. - Moist linings in hollow organs due to mucous secretion. - Lacks blood vessels, using diffusion for material exchange with connective tissue vessels. - Functions: - Absorption of water and nutrients. - Protection against injury, drying, infection, and harmful chemicals. - Elimination of waste products. - Types: - - Simple Epithelial Tissue: - Definition: - Single layer of cells on a basement membrane, found on secretory and absorptive surfaces. - Subtypes: - Squamous Epithelium: - Characteristics: - Single layer of flat, thin, disc-like cells with irregular boundaries, resembling floor tiles (pavement epithelium). - Flattened nuclei at cell centers, causing surface bulging. - Locations: - Skin, mouth lining, esophagus, terminal bronchioles, lung alveoli, membranous labyrinth, coelomic cavities, rete testis. - In blood vessels/heart: endothelium; in coelom: mesothelium. - Functions: - Protection, secretion of coelomic fluid, gas exchange. - Cuboidal Epithelium: - Characteristics: - Tall, polygonal, cube-shaped cells with central nuclei. - Microvilli on absorptive surfaces (e.g., small intestine) increase surface area. - Locations: - Small salivary glands, pancreatic ducts, thyroid vesicles, kidney distal convoluted tubules, ovaries, seminiferous tubules, testes, iris, lens inner surface, retina pigment layer. - Functions: - Protection, secretion, absorption, excretion, gamete formation. - Columnar Epithelium: - Characteristics: - Elongated cells like tall columns, with slightly broader outer surfaces. - Nuclei elongated, near cell bases; some cells (goblet cells) secrete mucus. - Locations: - Stomach, intestine, gall bladder, bile duct linings; gastric, intestinal, pancreatic glands. - Functions: - Protection, secretion, absorption of digestive juices. - Glandular Epithelium: - Characteristics: - Columnar or cuboidal cells specialized for secretion, found in glands. - Types: - Unicellular Glands: - Isolated glandular cells (e.g., goblet cells in intestine, mucous membrane). - Multicellular Glands: - Clusters of glandular cells (e.g., salivary glands). - Subtypes: - Tubular: - Tube-shaped (e.g., sweat glands, oil glands). - Alveolar: - Sac-like with obvious lumen (e.g., salivary glands, mammary glands). - Functions: - Secretion. - Ciliated Epithelium: - Characteristics: - Cubical or columnar cells with cilia (hair-like outgrowths) from basal granules. - Locations: - Respiratory tract, fallopian tubes, brain ventricles, spinal cord central canal. - Functions: - Protection, movement of mucus, urine, eggs, cerebrospinal fluid. - Additional Notes: - With mucous cells and connective tissue, forms mucosa/mucous membrane. - Cilia movement pushes mucus in the respiratory tract. - Pseudo-Stratified Epithelium: - Characteristics: - Single layer appearing multilayered due to unequal cell sizes; all cells rest on basement membrane. - Long cells: - columnar, ciliated, with oval nuclei, reach free surface. - Short cells: - round nuclei, non-ciliated, do not reach free surface. - Locations: - Large ducts, parotid salivary gland, male urethra, olfactory mucosa, trachea, large bronchi. - Functions: - Protection, secretion, movement of gland secretions, urine, semen, dust-laden mucus. - Compound Epithelial Tissue (Multilayered): - Definition: - Comprises multiple cell layers, thicker and stronger than simple epithelia, with minimal secretion/absorption roles but significant in protection against chemical, mechanical, thermal, or osmotic stresses. - Subtypes: - Stratified Epithelium: - Characteristics: - Multiple layers; deepest layer is loose columnar/cuboidal, outer layer varies. - Types Based on Outer Layer: - Stratified Squamous - Stratified Cuboidal - Stratified Columnar - Stratified Ciliated Columnar - Functions: - Protection. - Transitional Epithelium (Urothelium): - Characteristics: - 4–6 layers; basal layer columnar/cuboidal, middle layer polyhedral/pear-shaped, surface layer large, globular/umbrella-shaped. - Locations: - Renal calyces, renal pelvis, ureter, urinary bladder, part of urethra. - Functions: - Permits distention, allowing stretching (e.g., urinary bladder) without damage, regaining original shape. - Connective Tissue: - Overview: - Most abundant and widely distributed, arising from embryonic mesoderm. - Links and supports other tissues/organs. - Comprises intercellular medium, loosely packed living cells, and fibers in a matrix (jelly-like, dense, or rigid). - Functions: - Attachment, support (via cartilages/bones), storage, insulation (fat), material transport. - Everyday Science Notes: - Camel humps: - adipose tissue (fatty), conducts heat slowly, maintains body temperature, provides energy when food is scarce. - Laborers’ thick skin: - thickened epidermis, dermis, subcutaneous layers on palms/soles. - Bones: - small bones are solid; long bones (e.g., humerus, femur) have hollow bone marrow cavities. - Blood: - ~6.8 L in adults, 6–10% of body weight. - Types: - Vascular Connective Tissue: - Characteristics: - Cells suspended in a liquid matrix, no fibers. - Subtypes: - Lymph: - Pale yellow, contains plasma and WBCs; lacks RBCs and platelets. - Blood: - Mobile tissue with plasma and all blood corpuscles. - Connective Tissue Proper: - Characteristics: - More intercellular material than cells, with structural glycoproteins, glycosaminoglycans; soft matrix with cells and fibers. - Subtypes: - Reticular Connective Tissue: - Star-shaped reticular cells. - Pigmented Connective Tissue: - Irregular pigment cells (chromatophores). - Loose Connective Tissue: - Loosely arranged cells and fibers. - Subtypes: - Areolar Tissue: - Small spaces between fine threads, forms body framework. - Adipose/Fatty Tissue: - Fat-storing adipocytes; acts as shock absorber, heat insulator. - Types: - White (Yellow) Fat: - Monolocular, single large fat globule, less energetic. - Brown Fat: - Multilocular, multiple small fat globules, contains iron-based cytochrome pigment, more energetic. - Dense Connective Tissue: - Compactly arranged cells and fibers. - Subtypes: - Dense Irregular - Dense Regular: - White Fibrous Connective Tissue: - Collagen-dominant, tough, inelastic (e.g., tendons). - Yellow Elastic Connective Tissue: - Loose network of elastic, branched yellow fibers (e.g., ligaments). - Skeletal Connective Tissue: - Characteristics: - Forms endoskeleton, supports/protects body, rigid matrix with few cells/fibers. - Subtypes: - Bone: - Hard, non-pliable, contains osteoblasts, osteocytes, osteoclasts. - Matrix: - 62% inorganic, 38% organic (ossein). - Cartilage: - Soft, avascular, contains chondrocytes, chondrin (matrix), aggrecan (core protein). - Subtypes: - Hyaline Cartilage: - Clear, elastic matrix with few fibers; found in articular surfaces, embryonic skeleton. - White Fibrocartilage: - Firm matrix with white fibers; strongest, found in intervertebral discs. - Elastic Cartilage: - Matrix with yellow fibers; found in pinna, eustachian tube. - Calcified Cartilage: - Matrix with calcium carbonate granules; hard, found in shark vertebral column. - Muscular Tissue: - Overview: - Composed of myocytes (muscle cells) with contractility, capable of shortening and returning to relaxed state. - Exhibits electrical excitability due to plasma membrane potential difference. - Structurally: - long, narrow cells (muscle fibers) with sarcoplasm (cytoplasm), sarcoplasmic reticulum (endoplasmic reticulum), myofibrils (protein threads), abundant mitochondria (sarcosomes), and glycogen granules for energy via glucose oxidation. - Functions: - Movement of body parts and locomotion. - Supports bones and structures. - Facilitates heartbeat, blood/lymph flow, food movement in alimentary canal, air flow in respiratory tract, sound production, waste propulsion. - Enables facial expressions and gestures. - Types: - Striated (Striped) Muscle: - Characteristics: - Comprises 80%+ of vertebrate soft tissue mass; long, cylindrical, unbranched, multinucleate cells with alternating light/dark strips. - Locations: - Body walls, limbs, tongue, pharynx, esophagus beginning; forms muscles (e.g., biceps, triceps) in bundles wrapped by connective tissue. - Features: - Rapid contraction, fatigues quickly. - Innervated by brain/spinal cord nerves; voluntary (conscious control). - Joined to bones by tendons; contains blood capillaries. - Non-Striated (Smooth) Muscle: - Characteristics: - Thin, long, spindle-shaped, uninucleate cells with central conical nuclei, arranged in parallel branched bundles. - Myofilaments: - primary (myosin), secondary (actin, tropomyosin, troponin). - Types: - Single Unit: - In walls of hollow organs (e.g., digestive tract, uterus, ureters, urinary bladder). - Multi Unit: - In dermis, iris, large blood vessel walls. - Locations: - Iris, viscera (alimentary canal, genital tracts, ducts, blood vessels, urinary bladder, ureter); also called visceral muscles. - Features: - Slow contraction, sustained without fatigue; involuntary (not under conscious control). - Cardiac Muscle: - Characteristics: - Cylindrical, branched, uninucleate cells (1–2 nuclei) confined to heart walls. - Functions: - Generates self-propagating excitation waves for rhythmic heart contractions; involuntary. - Neural Tissue: - Overview: - Receives stimuli and conducts impulses for body control and coordination. - Forms central nervous system (CNS), peripheral nervous system (PNS), autonomic nervous system (ANS). - Composed of nerve cells and glial cells. - Function: - Coordinates organ functions based on specific needs. - Components: -
ComponentDescription
NeuronsStructural and functional units, longest body cells, with cyton (cell body), axon (single long part), dendrites (small branched parts).
Neurosecretory CellsSpecialized nerve cells acting as endocrine organs, releasing neurohormones into blood instead of synaptic clefts.
NeurogliaNon-nervous cells in CNS, ganglia, retina; hold neurons in place; include microglia and macroglia.
Ependymal CellsEpithelial layer lining brain ventricles and spinal cord central canal, with microvilli (for cerebrospinal fluid absorption) and cilia.
- Additional Notes: - Organs like brain, spinal cord, and nerves are composed of neural tissues. - Nutrition - Definition: - The process by which organisms intake nutrients (carbohydrates, fats, proteins, minerals, vitamins, water) and utilize them for life processes such as growth, repair, and reproduction. - Essential for providing energy for vital activities required for survival. - Nutrients - Definition: - Chemical substances obtained from the environment, used as energy sources for metabolic processes or for biosynthesis of body constituents (e.g., tissues, organs). - Can be simple or complex organic molecules (e.g., proteins), inorganic molecules (e.g., CO₂), or ions (e.g., nitrate (NO₃⁻), potassium (K⁺), calcium (Ca²⁺), zinc (Zn²⁺)). - Types of Nutrients: - Macronutrients: - Required in large amounts, provide calories, support growth and metabolism. - Nine in number: - Carbon (C), Hydrogen (H), Oxygen (O), Nitrogen (N), Phosphorus (P), Potassium (K), Sulfur (S), Magnesium (Mg), Calcium (Ca). - Micronutrients: - Required in trace amounts, aid in detoxifying the body and combating diseases. - Examples: - Zinc (Zn), Iron (Fe), Chlorine (Cl). - Essential Nutrients: - Cannot be synthesized by the body, must be obtained externally. - Eight in number: - Iron (Fe), Zinc (Zn), Boron (B), Manganese (Mn), Copper (Cu), Molybdenum (Mo), Chlorine (Cl). - Non-essential Nutrients: - Can be synthesized by the body, sometimes obtained externally. - Examples: - Cobalt (Co), Silicon (Si), Sodium (Na), Aluminum (Al), Lead (Pb). - Nutrition in Plants - Overview: - Plants require essential nutrients for growth and maintenance. - They synthesize food via photosynthesis using carbon dioxide (CO₂), water (H₂O), and sunlight. - Types of Nutrition in Plants: - - Autotrophic Nutrition: - Definition: - Organisms (autotrophs) synthesize their own food from simple substances (CO₂, H₂O) using sunlight through photosynthesis. - The term "auto" means self, "trophe" means nutrition. - Examples: - Green plants, blue-green algae, some bacteria, most unicellular organisms. - Subtypes: - Photoautotrophic Nutrition: - Organisms (photoautotrophs) use light energy to prepare food. - Examples: - Green plants, purple, red, green bacteria. - Chemoautotrophic Nutrition: - Organisms (chemoautotrophs) use chemical energy from oxidation of inorganic substances. - Examples: - Nitrifying bacteria, iron bacteria, sulfur bacteria. - Heterotrophic Nutrition: - Definition: - Organisms (heterotrophs) cannot synthesize food from inorganic materials (e.g., CO₂, H₂O) and depend on other organisms. - Common in plants lacking chlorophyll, using chemical bond energy of organic molecules. - The term "heteros" means others, "trophe" means nutrition. - Subtypes: - Parasitic Plants: - Derive some or all nutrients from living host plants using specialized organs (haustoria) to extract water and nutrients. - Examples: - Cuscuta, Orobanche, Viscum, Loranthus, Santalum, Hyobanche, Sanguinea, dodder, Rafflesia, Western Australian Christmas tree. - Saprophytic Plants: - Grow on dead/decaying animal or plant matter, secreting digestive juices to convert it into a nutrient solution. - Known as saprotrophs. - Examples: - Fungi, bacteria. - Symbiotic Plants: - Form mutualistic relationships with other plants for nutritional benefits, with no adverse effects on either partner. - Example: - Bryophytes with aerial roots attach to tree bark for anchorage and height in the canopy. - Insectivorous Plants (Carnivorous Angiosperms): - Obtain nutrients by trapping/consuming insects or arthropods, often using pitcher-like structures (modified leaves) with lids and downward hairs. - Release enzymes to digest trapped organisms, supplying nitrogen in nutrient-poor environments. - Examples: - Pitcher plant, Drosera, Venus flytrap, Utricularia, Dionaea. - Mineral Nutrition in Plants: - Definition: - Study of the source, absorption, distribution, and metabolism of inorganic substances (minerals) for growth, development, structure, physiology, and reproduction. - Mineral Nutrients, Functions, and Deficiency Symptoms: -
Mineral ElementObtained AsFunctionsDeficiency Symptoms
NitrogenNO₃⁻, NO₂⁻, NH₄⁺Constituent of proteins, nucleic acids, vitamins, hormones.Chlorosis, premature leaf fall.
PhosphorusH₂PO₄⁻, HPO₄²⁻Constituent of cell membranes, proteins, nucleic acids, nucleotides; required for phosphorylation.Delayed seed germination, purple spots on older leaves, premature leaf fall.
MagnesiumMg²⁺Activates enzymes in respiration/photosynthesis, involved in DNA/RNA synthesis, constituent of chlorophyll’s ring structure, maintains ribosome structure.Chlorosis, necrosis.
CalciumCa²⁺Used in cell wall synthesis during division, forms mitotic spindle, maintains cell membrane function, regulates metabolism.Necrosis of young meristematic regions, stunted growth.
PotassiumK⁺Maintains ionic balance, aids protein synthesis, enzyme activation, stomatal opening/closing, cell turgidity.Scorched leaf tips, loss of cambial activity, loss of apical dominance.
SulfurSO₄²⁻Present in cysteine, methionine; constituent of coenzymes, vitamins, ferredoxin.Chlorosis, stunted growth, anthocyanin accumulation.
IronFe³⁺Constituent of proteins (e.g., ferredoxin, cytochromes) for electron transfer, activates catalase, aids chlorophyll formation.Interveinal chlorosis.
ManganeseMn²⁺Activates enzymes in photosynthesis, respiration, nitrogen metabolism; splits water to liberate oxygen in photosynthesis.Chlorosis, grey spots on leaves.
ZincZn²⁺Activates enzymes (e.g., carboxylases), aids auxin synthesis.Chlorosis, leaf malformation.
CopperCu²⁺Supports overall metabolism, associated with enzymes in redox reactions.Necrosis of leaf tips, dieback of shoots.
BoronBO₃³⁻, B₄O₇²⁻Supports Ca²⁺ uptake/utilization, membrane function, pollen germination, cell elongation/differentiation, carbohydrate metabolism.Premature leaf abscission, stunted growth, disintegration of internal leaves.
MolybdenumMoO₂²⁻Component of enzymes (e.g., nitrogenase, nitrate reductase).Defective nitrogen metabolism, chlorosis.
ChlorineCl⁻Determines solute concentration, maintains ionic balance, essential for water-splitting in photosynthesis.Wilting, chlorosis, necrosis.
- Nitrogen Fixation: - Definition: - Process converting atmospheric nitrogen (N₂) into compounds via abiological or biological processes, independent of respiration. - Types: - Abiological Nitrogen Fixation: - Nitrogen combines with oxygen during electric discharge to form nitrogen oxides. - Biological Nitrogen Fixation: - Atmospheric nitrogen (N₂) is converted to ammonia (NH₃) using the enzyme nitrogenase, utilized by plants. - Reaction: - \( N_2 + 8H^+ + 8e^- \rightarrow 2NH_3 + H_2 \) - Nitrogen as a Nutrient: - Required in large quantities for synthesis of nucleic acids, proteins, and nitrogenous compounds. - Plants cannot use gaseous nitrogen (78% of atmosphere); obtain it as nitrite (NO₂⁻), nitrate (NO₃⁻), or ammonium (NH₄⁺). - Microorganisms Involved: - Cyanobacteria: - Utilize inorganic/organic nitrogen sources (e.g., nitrate, nitrite, ammonium, urea, amino acids). - Legume Family (Fabaceae): - Host symbiotic Rhizobia bacteria in root nodules (e.g., kudzu, clovers, soybeans, alfalfa, lupines, peanuts). - Lichens: - Lobaria, Peltigera. - Others: - Mosquito ferns, cycads, Gunnera. - Nutrition in Animals - Definition: - Process by which animals use/absorb nutrients for growth, development, maintenance, and cellular/metabolic activities. - Types of Nutrition in Animals: - Holozoic Nutrition: - Involves engulfing, digesting, and absorbing food. - Subtypes: - Herbivorous: - Animals (herbivores) consume plants or plant products. - Examples: - Cow, buffalo, horse, goat, deer, sheep. - Carnivorous: - Animals (carnivores) consume other animals. - Examples: - Fox, lion, tiger, leopard. - Scavengers: - Feed on dead remains of organisms. - Examples: - Ant, kite, vulture. - Omnivorous: - Consume both plants and animals. - Examples: - Humans. - Parasitic Nutrition: - One animal (parasite) depends on another (host) for nutrition. - Subtypes: - External Parasites: - Attach to host’s outer surface for nutrition. - Examples: - Bug, leech, mosquito. - Internal Parasites: - Live inside host’s body for nutrition. - Examples: - Roundworm, ringworm. - Saprozoic Nutrition: - Animals extract food from decomposed, non-living organic matter as solutions. - Examples: - Insects in drainage, mushrooms on rotting matter, microbes on pickles/bread. - Nutrients in Animals: - Carbohydrates: - Include monosaccharides, disaccharides, polysaccharides. - Fats: - Include animal fats, vegetable fats, saturated/unsaturated fatty acids. - Proteins - Vitamins: - Water-soluble, fat-soluble. - Water: - Comprises ~65% of human body, ~70% in protoplasm. - Does not yield energy but is vital; deprivation leads to death. - Functions: - Regulates body temperature. - Medium for excretory activities. - Facilitates biochemical reactions in aqueous environments. - Everyday Science: - Average adult needs 5–6 glasses of water daily, varying with temperature, humidity, and physical activity. - Infants under 6 months should not consume water due to immature kidneys. - Roughage: - Indigestible fibrous material (e.g., cellulose in plant cell walls from vegetables, fruits, bran). - Provides bulk to satisfy appetite, expands gut, stimulates peristalsis for fecal elimination. - Everyday Science: - Prevents constipation; not digested in human intestines. - Beneficial bacteria (e.g., E. coli) synthesize vitamin K in the gut. - Pregnant women advised to consume folic acid-rich foods (e.g., green vegetables) for DNA synthesis in first trimester. - Minerals (Inorganic Salts): - Constitute ~4% of body weight, required in small amounts (≤1 g/day). - Control metabolic activities. - Must be consumed as compounds, as elemental forms (e.g., sodium, potassium) can be fatal. - Daily Requirements: -

- Functions and Deficiency Symptoms: - Macrominerals: -

Calcium (Ca)Dairy, eggs, wheat germForms bones/teeth, aids blood clotting, maintains muscle/nerve activity.Rickets, muscular spasms, nervous disorders.
Chlorine (Cl)Table salt, seafood, chlorinated waterMaintains pH balance, constituent of stomach HCl.pH imbalance, stomach ulcers.
Magnesium (Mg)Dairy, cereals, green vegetables, seafood, wheat germ, chocolateAids muscle/nerve relaxation, bone/teeth synthesis.Convulsions, hallucinations.
Phosphorus (P)Dairy, eggs, fish, meat, wheat germForms bones/teeth, biomembranes, nucleic acids, ATP; maintains muscle/nerve activity.Bone mineral loss, metabolic/cardiac/nerve disorders.
Sodium (Na)Table salt, most foods, wheat germSupports muscle activity, nerve impulse transmission.Cramps, diarrhea, dehydration.
Potassium (K)Cereals, meats, fruits, vegetablesAids nerve impulse conduction with sodium.-
Sulfur (S)Dairy, eggs, meatPart of proteins, vitamin B1; supports hair, skin, nail health.Poor hair/nail growth.

- Everyday Science: - Chocolates: High nickel content, potentially harmful. - Milk: High nutritive value but poor iron source. - Fluoride: In toothpaste, enhances bacterial resistance, prevents tooth decay. - Acidic foods (e.g., tomato sauce, orange juice, tea, coffee): Leach lead from plates/containers. - High salt intake: Causes water retention, facial puffiness. - Balanced Diet: - Definition: - A diet containing all essential nutrients in appropriate amounts to meet metabolic needs (energy, growth, replacement, physical regulation). - Components: - Energy/fuel foods, building foods, minerals, green/yellow vegetables, vitamins, roughage, water. - Note: - Costly food does not guarantee a balanced diet. - Plant Morphology and Physiology - Plant Morphology - Definition: - Study of the outer forms and features of plant organs, including roots, stems, leaves, flowers, fruits, and seeds. - Plant Diversity: - Overview: - Plants exhibit diversity in form, size, lifespan, habit, habitat, and nutrition. - Types of Plants: - Herbs: - Small plants with soft, pliable stems, typically under 2 meters in height. - Examples: - Wheat, buttercup, henbane, canna. - Shrubs: - Medium-height plants with perennial woody stems, also called bushes. - Examples: - Capparis, jasmine, rose. - Trees: - Tall plants with a thick woody stem (trunk). - Examples: - Palm, Pinus, Casuarina, Eucalyptus, Dalbergia, Banyan. - Trailers: - Spread over the ground without rooting of prostrate stems. - Examples: - Tribulus, Euphorbia. - Creepers: - Have prostrate stems with roots at intervals. - Examples: - Grass. - Twiners: - Weak-stemmed plants with stems that twine around supports. - Examples: - Ipomoea. - Climbers: - Rise up supports using special clinging or coiling structures. - Examples: - Grape vine. - Epiphytes: - Live on other plants for space, not nutrition. - Examples: - Vanda. - Plant Parts: - - Overview: - Flowering plants consist of a cylindrical axis divided into an underground root system and an aerial shoot system. - Root system: - Comprises roots and lateral branches. - Shoot system: - Includes stem, branches, and leaves. - Vegetative organs: - Roots, stems, leaves. - Reproductive organs: - Flowers, fruits, seeds (covered in the reproduction chapter). - Root: - Definition: - Non-green, underground part developing from the radicle, growing downward into the soil. - Characteristics: - Descending portion of the plant axis, grows downward in soil. - Does not perform photosynthesis. - Root apex is sub-apical, protected by a parenchymatous multicellular root cap. - Contains unicellular root hairs that increase surface area for water absorption. - Not divided into nodes or internodes. - Types of Root Systems: - Adventitious Root System: - Develops from parts other than the radicle, common in monocots. - Modifications: - Storage of Food: - Roots swell due to food storage, classified by shape and swelling location: - Tuberous Roots: - e.g., sweet potato (\( Ipomoea\ batatas \)). - Fasciculated Roots: - e.g., Dahlia, Asparagus. - Palmate Tuberous Roots: - e.g., Orchis. - Nodulose Roots: - e.g., mango ginger (\( Curcuma\ amada \)), turmeric. - Moniliform Roots: - e.g., \( Dioscorea\ alata \), Indian spinach (\( Basella\ rubra \)), bitter gourd (\( Momordica\ charantia \)). - Annulated Roots: - e.g., ipecac (\( Psychotria\ ipecacuanha \)). - Mechanical Support: - Buttress Roots: - Radiate from tree base for support, e.g., \( Ficus \), \( Bombax \), \( Terminalia \), \( Salmalia \). - Stilt/Brace Roots: - Develop from basal stem nodes, fix plant in soil, e.g., sugarcane (\( Saccharum\ officinarum \)), maize (\( Zea\ mays \)), \( Pandanus\ tectorius \). - Prop/Pillar Roots: - Grow downward from horizontal branches, thicken in soil, e.g., banyan. - Contractile Roots: - Thick, fleshy, contractile, associated with underground stems, e.g., \( Crocus \). - Climbing/Clinging Roots: - Arise from nodes, secrete cementing substance for climbing, e.g., \( Ficus\ pumila \), betel. - Vital/Physiological Functions: - Assimilatory/Photosynthetic Roots: - Develop chlorophyll, perform photosynthesis, e.g., \( Trapa \), \( Tinospora \), \( Podostemon \). - Parasitic/Haustorial Roots: - Penetrate host tissue for nourishment, e.g., \( Cuscuta \) on \( Duranta \), \( Acacia \). - Epiphytic Roots: - Hang in air, absorb hygroscopic water via velamen tissue, e.g., \( Vanda \), \( Dendrobium \), orchids. - Reproductive Roots: - Aid vegetative reproduction, e.g., sweet potato, parwal. - Floating Roots: - Arise from nodes in floating plants, store air, become spongy, act as floats, e.g., \( Jussiaea \). - Tap Root System: - Develops from the radicle, common in dicots. - Modifications: - Storage Roots: - Swollen for storing food, classified by shape: - Fusiform: - Spindle-shaped, thick in middle, tapers at ends, e.g., radish. - Napiform: - Globular/spherical, thick at base, tapers at apex, e.g., beet (\( Beta\ vulgaris \)). - Conical: - Broad at base, tapers to apex, cone-shaped, e.g., carrot. - Tuberous Tap Root: - No specific shape, e.g., 4 o’clock plant (\( Mirabilis\ jalapa \)), parwal (\( Trichosanthes \)), \( Echinocystis\ lobata \). - Nodulated (Tuberculate) Roots: - Bear nodule-like swellings with nitrogen-fixing bacteria (\( Rhizobium\ leguminosarum \)) to convert atmospheric nitrogen to ammonia, e.g., groundnut, pea, clover. - Respiratory Roots (Pneumatophores): - Found in mangrove/swamp plants, have breathing pores (pneumatophores/lenticels) on negatively geotropic roots for oxygen uptake, e.g., \( Rhizophora \), sundari, \( Sonneratia \), \( Avicennia \). - Functions of Roots: - Fixes plant to soil, providing stability. - Absorbs and transports water and mineral salts. - Stores food for use by plants and animals. - Stem: - Definition: - Ascending organ developing from the plumule of a germinating seed, bearing leaves, flowers, branches, and fruits in flowering plants. - Characteristics: - Erect, aerial, grows away from soil toward light. - Has a terminal bud for longitudinal growth. - In flowering plants, divided into nodes (leaf attachment points) and internodes (stem between nodes). - Young stems are green, capable of photosynthesis. - Types Based on Position: - Aerial Stem: - Grows above soil, e.g., lemons, roses. - Subaerial Stem: - Trails or creeps on ground, e.g., water plants, grass. - Underground Stem: - Grows below soil, e.g., banana, potato. - Modifications of Stem: - Underground Modifications: - Exist below soil surface, store food and nutrients. - Types: - Sucker: - e.g., banana. - Rhizome: - e.g., \( Alocasia\ indica \), ginger (\( Zingiber\ officinale \)), turmeric (\( Curcuma\ domestica \)), \( Saccharum \). - Corm: - e.g., elephant foot (kachalu), \( Freesia \) (saffron), \( Alocasia \), banana. - Tuber: - e.g., rose, potato (stem tuber). - Bulb: - e.g., onion, tulip, narcissus. - Subaerial Modifications: - Weak stems, partially subterranean, used for vegetative propagation. - Types: - Runner: - e.g., grass, \( Merremia \). - Stolon: - e.g., mint, jasmine, strawberry. - Offset: - e.g., water plants, \( Pistia \). - Sucker: - e.g., roses, gillyflower. - Aerial Modifications: - Modified for specific functions. - Types: - Stem Tendrils: - For support and climbing, e.g., \( Cucurbita \) (pumpkin), \( Lagenaria \) (bottle gourd), grape vine. - Stem Thorns: - For protection, reduce transpiration, e.g., \( Citrus \), \( Duranta \), \( Bougainvillea \), pomegranate. - Phylloclade: - For photosynthesis in xerophytic conditions, e.g., \( Cactus \), \( Opuntia \). - Bulbils: - For food storage and vegetative reproduction, e.g., \( Dioscorea\ bulbifera \). - Cladodes/Cladophylls: - For photosynthesis, e.g., \( Ruscus \), \( Asparagus \). - Functions of Stem: - Bears leaves to maximize sunlight exposure. - Conducts water and minerals from roots to leaves, flowers, and fruits. - Positions flowers for pollination and fertilization. - Branches provide support to plant parts. - Leaf: - Definition: - Green, flat, thin, expanded lateral appendage on the stem, emerging at a node with an axillary bud. - Phyllotaxy: - Arrangement of leaves on stem/branches. - Venation: - Pattern of veins/veinlets in lamina (parallel in monocots, reticulate in dicots). - Characteristics: - Contains an axillary bud. - Growth is limited. - Exogenous origin, develops from swollen leaf primordium at the growing apex. - Comprises leaf base, petiole, and lamina. - Modifications of Leaf: - Modified for functions beyond photosynthesis. - Types: - Leaf Tendrils: - Thread-like, coiling structures for climbing, e.g., wild pea, sweet pea, lentils. - Leaf Spines: - Modified for protection and reduced transpiration, e.g., \( Aloe \), barberry. - Leaf Hooks: - Terminal leaflets form stiff, claw-like hooks, e.g., \( Doxantha \). - Phyllodes: - Green, flattened petiole/rachis for photosynthesis, e.g., \( Acacia\ melanoxylon \), rhubarb. - Leaf Bladders: - Modified leaf segments in aquatic carnivorous plants to trap insects, e.g., bladderwort. - Leaf Pitcher: - Tube-like with hairs and lid to trap insects, base photosynthesizes, e.g., pitcher plant, Venus flytrap. - Succulent Leaves: - Fleshy, store water/food, e.g., \( Aloe \), \( Agave \), \( Bryophyllum \). - Scale Leaves: - Small, colorless, protect buds/soft organs, e.g., garlic, onion. - Coloured Leaves/Bracts: - Brightly colored to attract insects, e.g., \( Poinsettia \). - Functions of Leaf: - Performs photosynthesis. - Site of transpiration. - Stores food (e.g., onion leaf base). - Protects axillary/terminal buds from desiccation and injury. - Inflorescence: - Definition: - Arrangement and distribution of flowers on the floral axis. - Types: - Racemose (Indeterminate): - Main axis continues growing, does not end in a flower, flowers in acropetal succession. - Examples: - Raceme (mustard), panicle (goldmohar), corymb (candytuft), spike (bottlebrush), spikelet (wheat), catkin (mulberry), spadix (palm), umbel (coriander), capitulum/head (sunflower), strobile (hop). - Cymose (Determinate): - Main axis ends in a flower, peduncle stops growing, flowers in basipetal succession. - Examples: - Uniparous/monochasial cyme (grape vine, potato), biparous/dichasial cyme (night jasmine), polychasial/multiparous cyme (\( Dianthus \)), cymose corymb (\( Ixora \)), cymose head. - Compound/Special Types: - Thyrsus, cyathium (\( Euphorbia \)), mixed spadix (banana), verticillaster (\( Ocimum \)), scorpigerous cyme, hypanthodium (banyan), mixed panicle (\( Ligustrum \)), coenanthium (\( Dorstenia \)). - Plant Physiology - Definition: - Study of physio-chemical activities in plants, including transportation, mineral nutrition, photosynthesis, respiration, growth, development, and reproduction. - Plant-Water Relations: - Importance: - Water is essential for all physiological activities in plants. - Functions of Water: - Transports nutrients from soil to plant. - Major component of protoplasm (water with molecules/particles). - Acts as a solvent and coolant. - Varies in amount: e.g., watermelon (92% water), woody plants (less water). - Essential for seed survival and respiration, despite appearing dry. - Limits growth and productivity in agriculture due to high water demand. - Terms Related to Plant-Water Relations: - Water Potential: - Defined by Slayter and Taylor (1966) as the difference in free energy of water molecules in pure water versus other systems. - Pure water has the highest water potential, affected by solute and pressure potentials. - Solute Potential: - Reduction in water potential due to solutes in pure water. - Pressure Potential: - Pressure in an osmotic system due to water entry/exit. - Processes: - Imbibition: - Water absorption by solid particles (imbibant) without forming a solution, causing swelling. - Examples: - Wood swelling in water, seed coat rupture during germination. - Everyday Science: - Wooden doors/windows hard to open/close in rainy season due to imbibition. - Osmosis: - Movement of water from high to low concentration through a semi-permeable membrane (SPM), discovered by Pfeffer. - Affected by solute concentration. - Examples: - Hypotonic Solution: - High external water concentration, cell gains water via endosmosis, becomes turgid (e.g., raisin in water swells). - Isotonic Solution: - Equal water concentration inside/outside, no size change. - Hypertonic Solution: - Low external water concentration, cell loses water via exosmosis, becomes flaccid (e.g., raisin in salt solution shrinks). - Everyday Science: - Excess fertilizer without water causes plasmolysis via exosmosis, killing plants. - Plasmolysis: - Shrinkage of plant cell due to water loss via exosmosis. - Deplasmolysis: Swelling of plasmolyzed cells in hypotonic solution/distilled water. - Means of Transport: - Diffusion: - Movement of ions/molecules (solid, liquid, gas) from high to low concentration without energy. - Active Transport: - Uses ATP to move ions/molecules from low to high concentration. - Transport System in Plants: - Overview: - Flowering plants lack a circulatory system but transport materials via complex mechanisms. - Water absorbed by roots reaches all plant parts, including growing tips, for photosynthesis and growth. - Short-distance transport (e.g., minerals, organic nutrients, growth regulators) via cytoplasmic streaming/diffusion. - Long-distance transport (translocation) of water via xylem, food via phloem. - Transport of Water: - Involves diffusion, osmosis, and potential gradients for short distances (cell-to-cell, across membranes). - Long-distance transport involves root pressure and transpirational pull. - Processes of Water Loss: - Transpiration: - Evaporative loss of 90–95% of absorbed water as vapor, mainly via leaf stomata. - Stomata: - Tiny pores on leaf surfaces for water vapor diffusion and gas exchange (O₂, CO₂). - Guard cells regulate stomatal opening/closing via exosmosis/endosmosis. - Guttation: - Loss of liquid water drops via hydathodes (pores) on leaf margins/vein ends when root pressure is high and transpiration is low. - Occurs in early morning in herbaceous plants (e.g., rose, strawberry, tomato). - Water contains organic/inorganic substances. - Transport of Minerals: - Absorbed by roots, transported upward via xylem with water. - Multidirectional, targeting growing regions (apical/lateral meristems, young leaves, flowers, fruits, seeds, storage organs). - Remobilization from old to young parts. - Translocation of Organic Solutes: - Organic compounds (synthesized in leaves via photosynthesis) transported as starch solution via phloem. - Multidirectional to all plant parts. - Photosynthesis: - Definition: - Enzyme-regulated anabolic process synthesizing organic compounds from CO₂ and H₂O using sunlight, storing energy as sugars (carbohydrates). - Oxygen is a waste product. - Equation: - \( 6CO_2 + 12H_2O \xrightarrow[\text{enzymes, radiant energy (686 kcal)}]{\text{chlorophyll}} C_6H_{12}O_6 + 6O_2 \uparrow + 6H_2O \) - Primarily occurs in leaves. - Process: - Oxidation-reduction process: - O₂ evolved from H₂O dissociation, sugars/starch synthesized from CO₂. - Initiated by light absorption in chlorophyll-containing reaction centers. - Two Phases: - Photochemical Phase (Light/Hill Reaction): - Occurs in thylakoids (grana region), light-dependent. - Involves photolysis of water, producing assimilatory power (NADPH, ATP). - Electrons from water photolysis picked up by P680 (photosystem-II reaction center), follow non-cyclic or cyclic photophosphorylation. - Biosynthetic Phase (Dark/Blackman’s Reaction): - Occurs in chloroplast stroma, does not require light but uses ATP and NADPH from light reaction. - Pathways: - Calvin cycle (C3), Hatch-Slack cycle (C4), Crassulacean Acid Metabolism (CAM). - Factors Affecting Photosynthesis: - Carbon Dioxide: - Up to 0.05% concentration increases rate; beyond this, no further effect. - Light: - Intensity, quality, duration affect rate. - Low intensity reduces rate; higher intensity increases it. - Effective in blue-violet (470–500 nm) and red (650–760 nm) spectrum; violet least effective. - Red light favors carbohydrate accumulation, blue light stimulates protein synthesis. - Visible light (380–760 nm) used; artificial light possible. - Water: - Small amount used in photosynthesis; scarcity reduces rate, closes stomata, limits CO₂ availability, causes wilting. - Temperature: - Affects dark reaction, not light reaction. - Optimal: - 10–25°C for C3 plants, 30–45°C for C4 plants. - Oxygen: - Small amount essential; high concentrations reduce rate (Warburg effect). - Plant Growth and Development: - Overview: - Initiates from a zygote, forming all structures (roots, stems, leaves, flowers, fruits, seeds) in an orderly sequence. - Growth is indeterminate (unlimited) due to continuous meristematic cell division (open growth). - Phases: - Cell division, cell enlargement, cell maturation. - Conditions/Factors for Growth: - Supply of nutrients. - C/N ratio (carbohydrates to nitrogenous compounds regulates growth patterns). - Temperature. - Water. - Light. - Oxygen. - Plant Growth Hormones (Phytohormones): - Definition: - Small, simple organic molecules produced naturally in higher plants, controlling growth and physiological functions in small amounts. - Synthesized in apical meristems/young leaves, transported via diffusion/phloem. - Act synergistically or antagonistically. - Types: - Auxins: - Natural: Indole-3-acetic acid (IAA); Synthetic: Naphthalene acetic acid (NAA), 2,4-dichlorophenoxyacetic acid (2,4-D). - Functions: - Promotes cell elongation, longitudinal growth. - Stimulates cambium cell division. - Enhances root growth/initiation. - Prevents abscission of leaves/fruits. - Induces parthenocarpy (seedless fruit formation). - Inhibits flowering generally, but promotes uniform flowering in pineapple. - Gibberellins: - Discovered by Kurosawa (1926); GA3 (gibberellic acid) most studied. - Functions: - Promotes stem elongation. - Reverses dwarfism. - Breaks dormancy in potato tubers, promotes seed germination (lettuce, cereals). - Induces parthenocarpy in apple, pear. - Controls flowering in long-day plants. - Regulates fruit growth/development. - Delays senescence in fruits (e.g., citrus, apples). - Cytokinins: - Discovered by Miller (1955); zeatin (from maize grains) most active. - Functions: - Promotes cell division, minimal effect on growth. - Breaks seed dormancy, promotes germination. - Stimulates lateral bud growth, suppresses apical dominance. - Delays senescence by controlling protein synthesis. - Induces parthenocarpy. - Extends shelf life of vegetables, cut shoots, flowers. - Ethylene: - Gaseous hormone from methionine, discovered by Burg (1962), known as ripening hormone. - Functions: - Inhibits growth. - Promotes fruit growth/ripening. - Induces flowering in pineapple, mango. - Inhibits lateral bud growth, promotes apical dominance. - Stimulates root initiation, lateral root/root hair growth. - Promotes leaf yellowing/senescence. - Breaks bud/seed dormancy. - Abscisic Acid: - Discovered by Addicott and Corns (1965), slightly acidic, growth inhibitor. - Functions: - Inhibits growth/metabolism. - Inhibits seed development/germination. - Causes senescence via chlorophyll loss. - Reduces transpiration by closing stomata. - Maintains seed/bud dormancy. - Promotes abscission of leaves, fruits, flowers. - Other Hormones: - Florigens: - Control/trigger flowering, produced in leaves, act in shoot apical meristems. - Callins: - Control organ growth, e.g., rhizocaline (promotes root formation, secreted by leaves). - Traumatin: - Produced in response to wounds in damaged cells. - Plant Diseases: - Definition: - Interruptions in normal plant functioning due to pathogens (bacteria, viruses, fungi) or mineral deficiencies. - Viral Diseases: -
DiseaseCausative OrganismSymptomsEffect on PlantPreventive Measures
Tobacco MosaicTobacco Mosaic Virus (TMV)Shrinking, mottled leaves, stunted leaves.Damages chlorophyll, flowers, fruits; stunts growth; reduces crop quality/quantity.Crop rotation, genetic engineering for resistant varieties.
Bunchy Top of BananaBanana Virus 1Narrow, dwarf, yellow, flat leaves; bunchy apex.Affects fruit/foliage, reduces growth, chokes leaves.Destroy affected plants (no cure).
Necrosis in PotatoesPotato Virus X (PVX)Sharp spots, dwarf leaves.Yield reduced >10%.Clonal selection, cultivate PVX-resistant varieties.
Potato Leaf RollPotato Leaf Roll VirusUpright, pale yellow leaves, rolling of leaflet bases.-Select healthy plants, rogue diseased plants.
Potato MosaicPotato Virus XSharp spots, dwarf leaves.Stunted plants, crinkled leaves.Crop rotation, genetic engineering for resistant varieties.
- Fungal Diseases: -
DiseaseCausative OrganismSymptomsEffect on PlantPreventive Measures
Loose Smut of Wheat\( Ustilago\ tritici \)Seed-transmitted, affects inflorescence, fruit wall bursts, exposes spores.Seeds affected, transmit disease.Use disease-resistant seeds, chemical control with triazole fungicides.
Powdery Mildew of Wheat\( Blumeria\ graminis \ f.sp.\ tritici \)White powdery mycelium/conidia on foliage, chlorosis on lower leaves.-Alter planting density, control nitrogen application, use fungicides (triadimefon, propiconazole), silicon/calcium silicate slag treatment.
Early Blight\( Alternaria\ solani \)Brown, angular, necrotic spots with concentric rings on leaves.Tuber rot, defoliation, early death.Organic fungicide spray.
Late Blight of Potato/Tomato\( Phytophthora\ infestans \)Brown lesions on leaves/stems, turning black in favorable conditions.Entire plant destroyed, dies rapidly.Crop rotation, fungicide application, plant pathogen-free tubers.
Damping Off Seedlings\( Pythium\ sp. \)Fungal infection in roots from diseased soil.Seeds fail to grow or die post-emergence.Soil treatment with formalin, captan, biltox-50; seed treatment with ziram, chloranil, captan.
Wart Disease of Potato\( Synchytrium\ endobioticum \)Black string-like structures on tubers.Rotten tubers, prevents tuber production.Quarantine, chemicals (\( HgCl_2 \), \( CuSO_4 \)), disease-resistant species.
Rust of Wheat\( Puccinia\ graminis \ tritici \)Red-brown rust on stems/leaves.-Use sulfur, zineb, dithane M-45, disease-resistant varieties (e.g., Lerma, Roja Sonora-64).
Green Ear of Bajra\( Sclerospora\ graminicola \)Dwarf plants, shortened internodes, green fibers turn black.Floral parts fail to form ears or form malformed green structures.Alter planting dates, rouging, deep plowing, soil solarization, remove diseased residues, crop rotation, seed treatment with Agrosan GN (0.1%) + Thiram (0.4%), captan, ceresan.
Brown Leaf Spot of Rice\( Bipolaris\ oryzae \)Small, circular/oval brown lesions on seedlings.Distorted leaves, stunted seedlings.Use resistant varieties, silicon fertilizers, seed treatment with captan, thiram, chitosan, carbendazim, mancozeb.
Rice Blast\( Magnaporthe\ oryzae \)Lesions on all plant parts.Whole plant affected.Crop rotation, genetic resistance, chemical fungicides.
Red Rot of Sugarcane\( Colletotrichum\ falcatum \), \( Glomerella\ cingulata \)Drying leaves, brown/reddish-brown stripes at nodal regions.Reduced yield.Avoid susceptible varieties, treat with 0.1% carbendazim for 15–20 minutes.
Tikka Disease of Groundnut\( Cercosporium\ personatum \), \( Cercospora\ arachidicola \)Pale to dark brown/black necrotic spots on leaves.15–70% yield loss.Burn plant debris, seed dressing with fungicides (bentate, vitavax), foliage spray with Bordeaux mixture, dithane M-45, Benlate, Baristin.
- Other Fungal Diseases: - Ergot of bajra, wilt of arhar, stem gall of coriander, rust of linseed, coffee rust. - Bacterial Diseases: -
DiseaseCausative OrganismSymptomsEffect on PlantPreventive Measures
Bacterial Leaf Blight\( Xanthomonas\ oryzae \ pv.\ oryzae \)Water-soaked to yellowish stripes on leaf blades/tips, bacterial ooze.Wilting, yellowing, drying leaves, reduced yield, plant death.Remove weed hosts/volunteer seedlings, proper fertilizer use, seed treatment with bleaching powder/zinc sulfate, use copper compounds/antibiotics.
Bacterial Blight of Cotton\( Xanthomonas\ campestris \ pv.\ malvacearum \)Angular leaf spots with red/brown borders, spreading to veins.Yield loss >10%.Seed processing with Agrosan GN/ceresan (2.5 mg/kg).
Citrus Canker\( Xanthomonas\ axonopodis \ pv.\ citri \), \( pv.\ aurantifolii \)Lesions on leaves, fruit, stems, premature fruit drop.Shoot dieback, fruit drop, reduced yield.Exclusion, sanitation, 1% Bordeaux mixture before sowing, antibiotics (streptomycin, phytomycin), disease-resistant varieties.
Tundu Disease of Wheat\( Corynebacterium\ tritici \), \( Anguina\ tritici \ (Nematode)Yellow exudate on spikes, wrinkled/twisted leaves, pod lesions, leaf odor.Dwarfed plant, lower parts fade after bending.Sow disease-resistant seeds, apply Temik/aldicarb sulfone/carbofuran in infested fields.
Wilt of Potato (Ring Disease)\( Pseudomonas\ solanacearum \)Collapsed vascular system, sharp brown ring in xylem.Reduced yield, destroyed potatoes.Apply 0.02% streptocycline solution for 30 minutes, crop rotation.
- Reproduction - Definition: - Biological process by which organisms produce offspring, ensuring species continuity through a cycle of birth, growth, and death. - Types of Reproduction: - Asexual Reproduction: - Definition: - Single parent produces offspring without gamete formation, offspring are exact copies (clones) of the parent. - Characteristics: - Uniparental, involves only one parent. - Occurs via mitotic division of somatic cells (somatogenic reproduction). - No gamete formation or fusion. - Offspring are genetically identical to parents (clones). - Examples: - Plants, protozoans, protists, sponges, coelenterates, Planaria. - Note: - Absent in higher non-vertebrates and all vertebrates. - Sexual Reproduction: - Definition: - Involves two parents of opposite sex, with fusion of male and female gametes. - Characteristics: - Biparental, involves male and female. - Meiotic division during gamete formation, mitotic division during development. - Offspring are genetically different from parents. - Slower process compared to asexual reproduction. - Examples: - Higher plants, animals. - Events in Sexual Reproduction: - Pre-Fertilization Events: - Gametogenesis: Formation of gametes. - Homogametes/Isogametes: - Male and female gametes similar in appearance. - Heterogametes: - Male and female gametes morphologically distinct. - Copulation/Gamete Transfer: - Close association of male and female for gamete fusion. - Fertilization (Syngamy): - Fusion of male and female gametes to form a diploid zygote. - Types: - External Fertilization: - Occurs outside the body, e.g., aquatic organisms (algae, fish, amphibians). - Internal Fertilization: - Occurs inside the body, e.g., fungi, higher animals, most plants (bryophytes, pteridophytes, gymnosperms, angiosperms). - Post-Fertilization Events: - Zygote formation and embryogenesis (embryo development from zygote). - Animal Categories Based on Zygote Development: - Oviparous: - Zygote develops outside female body, eggs laid with hard calcareous shell, e.g., reptiles, birds. - Everyday Science: - King Cobra builds nests, lays 20–40 eggs, guards them during 60–90 day incubation. - Viviparous: - Zygote develops inside female body, young delivered after growth, e.g., humans, monkeys, most mammals (except monotremes like platypus, echidna). - Note: - Viviparous animals have higher offspring survival due to embryonic care/protection. - Reproduction in Lower Plants: - Asexual Reproduction: - Fragmentation: - Parent plant body breaks into pieces, each developing into a new plant. - Common in algae growing in running water, e.g., Spirogyra, Ulothrix. - Spore Formation: - Reproductive cells (spores) germinate into new colonies, produced in sporangia with thick protective walls. - Types of Spores: - Zoospores (motile): - e.g., Ulothrix, Oedogonium, Pythium, Achlya. - Sporangiospores (non-motile): - e.g., Mucor, Rhizopus. - Conidia (non-motile): - e.g., Penicillium, Aspergillus, Fusarium. - Cysts (thick-walled): - e.g., Vaucheria, Botrydium. - Sexual Reproduction: - Sex organs: - Unicellular in algae/fungi, multicellular in bryophytes. - In evolved algae: - Motile male gametes (antheridium), non-motile female gametes (archegonium). - Fertilization: - Mature archegonium swells with water, releases mucilaginous substance, attracting antherozoids for zygote formation. - Reproduction in Higher Plants (Angiosperms): - Asexual Reproduction: - Methods: - Apomixis: - Producing offspring without gamete fusion. - Agamospermy/Adventitious Embryogenesis: - Embryo forms from integument/nucellus cells, e.g., Citrus. - Recurrent Apomixis: - Diploid egg develops parthenogenetically, e.g., Allium. - Vegetative Propagation: - New plants from vegetative parts (roots, stems, leaves, meristems). - Natural Vegetative Propagation: - Roots: - Adventitious tuberous buds, e.g., sweet potato, Dahlia. - Underground Stems: - Rhizome: - e.g., ginger. - Bulb: - e.g., garlic. - Tuber eye (nodes): - e.g., potato. - Suckers: - e.g., mint. - Bulbils: - e.g., Agave, pineapple. - Leaves: - Adventitious buds: - e.g., Begonia. - Plantlets at margins: - e.g., Bryophyllum. - Buds at leaf tip: - e.g., Adiantum caudatum. - Artificial Vegetative Propagation: - Cutting: - e.g., Bryophyllum (leaves), tamarind (roots), grapes, sugarcane, rose (stems). - Layering: - e.g., jasmine, grapevine, litchi, orange. - Grafting: - e.g., rose, apple, plum, peach, mango. - Micropropagation (Tissue Culture): e.g., orchids, carnations, ornamental plants. - Tissue Culture: - Involves growing tissues/organs (isolated cells/small tissue pieces) in sterile nutrient cultures to produce disease-free plants. - Explant: - Small plant parts (flower buds, stem tissue, growing tips, leaves) form callus (undifferentiated cell mass). - Advantages: - Produces large numbers of disease-free plants. - Avoids plant diseases via sterile techniques. - Enables rapid production of ornamental plants. - Unaffected by climatic conditions. - Sexual Reproduction (Amphimixis): - - Occurs in flowers, with seeds enclosed in fruits. - Flower Types: - Bisexual: - Contain both male (androecium) and female (gynoecium) organs, e.g., lily, rose. - Unisexual: - Contain either male or female organs, e.g., papaya, watermelon. - Flower Parts: - Sepals: - Outermost, leaf-like, protect buds (calyx), e.g., fall off or remain as fleshy structures post-fertilization. - Petals: - Brightly colored, attract insects (corolla). - Stamen (Male Reproductive Part): - Filament: - Stalk supporting anther. - Anther: - Bilobed, contains pollen sacs with pollen grains. - Carpel/Pistil (Female Reproductive Part): - Stigma: - Sticky, receives pollen. - Style: - Connects stigma to ovary. - Ovary: - Basal, contains ovules with egg cells. - Pollination: - Transfer of pollen from anther to stigma. - Types: - Self-Pollination (Autogamy/Homogamy): - Pollen transfer within same flower, occurs in bisexual flowers, e.g., wheat, rice. - Cross-Pollination (Allogamy): - Pollen transfer between flowers (same or different plant), occurs in unisexual/bisexual flowers. - Agents of Pollination: - Anemophilous (wind): - e.g., mulberry, Typha, oak. - Entomophilous (insects): - e.g., Ficus, Salvia. - Hydrophilous (water): - e.g., Vallisneria spiralis. - Zoophilous (animals): - e.g., Rafflesia. - Ornithophilous (birds): - e.g., Bignonia, Salvia. - Malacophilous (snails): - e.g., Araceae family members. - Chiropterophilous (bats): - e.g., baobab tree. - Everyday Science: - Smallest flower: - Wolffia. - Largest flower: - Rafflesia. - National Flower of India: - Lotus (Nelumbium). - Fertilization (Syngamy): - - Pollen tube enters ovule via nucellus, deposits pollen grains in endosperm. - Process: - Microspore in anther’s microsporangia forms pollen grains with two male sperms. - Pollen reaches stigma, forms pollen tube to deliver sperms to embryo sac. - One sperm fuses with egg to form zygote (2n), another fuses with secondary nucleus to form primary endosperm nucleus (3n, triple fusion). - Double Fertilization: - Discovered by S.G. Nawaschin; first described by Strasburger in Monotrapa. - Triple Fusion: - Second sperm + secondary nucleus (2n) → primary endosperm nucleus (3n). - Post-Fertilization Events: - Zygote forms embryo; ovule becomes seed; ovary becomes fruit. - Fate of Flower Parts: -

- Fruit: - Formed from ovary via cell division/differentiation post-fertilization. - Pericarp: - Ovary wall, may be fleshy (e.g., guava, tomato, cucumber) or dry/leathery (e.g., pea, bean, mustard). - Types: - True Fruits: - Derived only from ovary, e.g., mango, tomato. - False Fruits: - Derived from ovary and accessory floral parts (e.g., thalamus), e.g., apple, cashew, strawberry. - Classification: - Simple Fruit: - e.g., mustard, bean, lemon, mango. - Aggregate Fruit: - e.g., raspberry, apple, strawberry. - Composite Fruit: - e.g., jackfruit, mulberry, banyan. - Edible Parts: -

- Seeds: - Fertilized mature ovule with an embryonic plant. - Types: - Non-Endospermic (Non-Albuminous): - No endosperm, food stored in cotyledons, e.g., gram, pea. - Endospermic (Albuminous): - Contains endosperm for food storage, e.g., castor, maize, rice. - Reproduction in Animals: - Asexual Reproduction: - Common in protists, cnidarians, tunicates. - Methods: - Regeneration: - Whole body forms from a fragment, e.g., Planaria, Hydra. - Spore Formation: - Propagules (e.g., zoospores, conidia) germinate into new individuals. - Fission: - Parent divides into two or more parts, each becoming a new individual, e.g., protozoans, planarians. - Budding: - Outgrowths (buds) develop into new organisms, e.g., yeast, coelenterates. - Strobilization: - Ring-like constrictions form, resembling stacked saucers, e.g., Aurelia. - Fragmentation: - Parent breaks into fragments, each becoming a new organism, e.g., sponges, echinoderms. - Gemmules: - Asexual reproductive structures in sponges, oval-shaped. - Sexual Reproduction: - - Involves fusion of haploid sperm and egg to produce diploid offspring. - Dioecious: - Separate male (produces sperm) and female (produces egg) individuals. - Monoecious (Hermaphrodites): - Single individual produces both sperm and egg, e.g., earthworms, snails. - Types: - Syngamy: - Permanent fusion of gametes. - Exogamy: - Fusion of gametes from different parents (dioecious). - Endogamy: - Fusion of gametes from the same parent (monoecious). - Isogamy: - Fusion of morphologically similar gametes, e.g., Monocystis. - Anisogamy: - Fusion of morphologically dissimilar gametes, e.g., frog, rabbit. - Conjugation: - Temporary fusion for nuclear material exchange, e.g., Paramecium, bacteria. - Reproduction in Humans: - Overview: - Humans are viviparous, unisexual, with gametes (sperms in males, ova in females) produced by gonads. - Sperm and ovum fuse to form a zygote, developing into a multicellular organism via mitotic division. - Puberty: - Age of sexual maturity. - Females: - Begins at 10–11 years, completes at 15–17 years. - Signs: - Breast development, pubic/armpit hair, menstruation onset. - Males: - Begins at 11–12 years, completes at 16–17 years. - Signs: - Pubic/armpit/facial hair, muscle growth, voice deepening, penis size increase. - - Menstrual Cycle: - Definition: - Cyclic changes in uterus/ovaries starting at puberty for sexual reproduction. - Menarche: - Start at 13–15 years; Menopause: Ceases at 45–50 years. - Cycle duration: - ~28–29 days. - Mechanisms in Human Reproduction: - Gametogenesis: - Formation of gametes (sperms in testes, ova in ovaries). - Types: - Spermatogenesis: - Occurs in seminiferous tubules of testes. - Spermatogonium (sperm mother cell) → 4 functional spermatozoa. - Process: - Spermatogonia → primary spermatocytes → secondary spermatocytes → spermatids → spermatozoa. - Sperm: - Minute, yolkless, motile. - Oogenesis: - Occurs in ovaries. - Oogonia → primary oocytes → one secondary oocyte + one polar body → one ootid + one polar body → one ovum. - Phases: - Multiplication, growth, maturation. - Ovum: - Large, often yolked, non-motile. - Polar bodies formed, unlike spermatogenesis. - Differences: -
AspectSpermatogenesisOogenesis
LocationTestesOvaries
Starting CellSpermatogonia → primary spermatocytesOogonia → primary oocytes
DivisionPrimary spermatocyte → 2 secondary spermatocytes → 4 spermatidsPrimary oocyte → 1 secondary oocyte + 1 polar body → 1 ootid + 1 polar body
Polar BodyNoneFormed
Output1 spermatogonium → 4 spermatozoa1 oogonium → 1 ovum
Gamete TraitsMinute, yolkless, motileLarge, often yolked, non-motile
- Fertilization: - Fusion of haploid sperm and ovum to form diploid zygote. - Internal, occurs in ampullary-isthmic junction of fallopian tube. - Ovum secretes glycoprotein fertilisin; sperm has antifertilisin protein for penetration. - Embryonic Development: - Organs form from three germ layers: - Ectoderm: - Outer epithelium (hair, nails, glands, mouth lining, tooth enamel, eye lens, inner ear, nasal/olfactory epithelium), neural tube (brain, spinal cord, motor nerves), neural crest (sensory ganglia/nerves, adrenal medulla, sympathetic ganglia, skull, gill arches, tooth dentine). - Mesoderm: - Notochord, thoracic/abdominal cavity lining, circulatory system (blood, bone marrow, lymphoid tissue, vessel endothelium), somites (skeletal muscle, bones, cartilage, dermis, connective tissues), urogenital system (ureter, kidney, gonads, ducts). - Endoderm: - Respiratory tract epithelium, pharynx (pouches, thyroid, parathyroid), liver, pancreas, urogenital epithelium. - Parturition: - Expulsion of fully formed fetus from uterus at gestation’s end. - Induced by neural/hormonal systems: - Oxytocin: - Promotes uterine muscle contraction (birth hormone). - Relaxin: - Increases pubic ligament flexibility, dilates uterine cervix during labor. - Fetal Ejection Reflex: - Mild uterine contractions from placenta when fetus is fully developed. - Lactation: - Milk production in mammary glands, starting late pregnancy/post-birth. - Colostrum: - First milk, rich in proteins, calories, IgA antibodies, provides passive immunity. - Prolactin: - Controls milk synthesis. - Oxytocin: - Controls milk release (pituitary gland). - Reproductive Health: - Definition: - State of physical, mental, social well-being in all reproductive aspects. - India’s Reproductive and Child Health Care (RCH) programs aim for total reproductive health. - Family planning program initiated in 1951. - Birth Control (Contraceptive) Methods: - Barrier Methods: -

- Sexually Transmitted Diseases (STDs): - Also called venereal diseases (VD) or reproductive tract infections (RTI), transmitted via sexual intercourse. -
