- Transgenic Animals: - Animals with foreign genes (e.g., mice for polio vaccine testing, human protein production). - Agriculture: - Aims to increase food production via: 1. Agrochemical-based: Pesticides/fertilizers (limited due to environmental harm). 2. Organic: - Enhances biodiversity, uses natural methods (e.g., rock phosphate, limestone). 3. Genetically Engineered Crops: - GM crops: - Tolerant to stress, pest-resistant, reduced post-harvest losses, improved nutrition. - Examples: - Papaya: - Resists ringspot virus. - Golden Rice: - Contains β-carotene (vitamin A precursor). - Flavr Savr Tomato: - Delayed ripening, stress-tolerant, nutritious. - Canola: - Edible oil, biodiesel. - Bt Cotton/Brinjal: - Bacillus thuringiensis gene for insect resistance. - Reproduction and Embryology: - In-vitro Fertilization (IVF): - Egg fertilized outside body, transferred to uterus (test-tube baby, Edwards/Steptoe). - Gamete Intra Fallopian Transfer (GIFT): - Eggs/sperm placed in Fallopian tubes for in-utero fertilization. - Zygote Intra Fallopian Transfer (ZIFT): - In-vitro fertilized egg placed in Fallopian tube (64.8% success rate). - Environmental Biotechnology: - Uses biological systems for environmental remediation, sustainable processes (e.g., biogas digesters, zero-waste agriculture). - Interrelated Fields: - Blue: - Marine/aquatic applications. - Green: - Agricultural processes. - Red: - Medical processes. - White: - Industrial processes. - Environment and Its Effects - Definition of Environment - Description: - The environment encompasses the surroundings and everything around us, including the composition of physical and biological conditions in which we live. - It includes physical components such as air (atmosphere), water (hydrosphere), and land (lithosphere), as well as biological components like plants, animals, human beings, and microorganisms (e.g., bacteria, fungi) and their interrelationships. - Human Impact: - Human activities significantly disturb the environmental balance to meet needs for food, clothing, housing, industry, transportation, etc. - Types of Environment - Physical Environment (Abiotic or Natural Environment): - Description: - Comprises land, water, and atmospheric conditions that contribute to soil formation. - Factors: - Temperature, pressure, moisture, precipitation, wind speed, rainwater, etc. - Biotic Environment (Organic Environment): - Description: - Responsible for the existence and survival of life forms. - Social or Cultural Environment: - Description: - Created by humans through social and cultural activities. - Includes historical, cultural, political, moral, and economic aspects of human life. - Atmosphere of Earth - Definition: - A gaseous layer surrounding the planet, retained by Earth’s gravity, protecting life forms from ultraviolet solar radiation and greenhouse gases. - Composition of Air: - Air is colorless, odorless, tasteless, and chemically composed of: - Nitrogen (\( N_2 \)): - 78.09% - Oxygen (\( O_2 \)): - 20.95% - Argon (\( Ar \)): - 0.93% - Carbon dioxide (\( CO_2 \)): - 0.039% - Trace amounts of other gases - Variable water vapor, averaging about 1% - Atmospheric Layers: - Description: - Air content and pressure vary with altitude, forming distinct layers. - Layers of the Atmosphere: -
Layer Altitude Range (km) Characteristics Temperature Range Key Components
Troposphere 0–18 Turbulent, dusty zone containing air, water vapor, and clouds. Suitable for terrestrial plants and animals. \( 15^\circ C \) to \( -50^\circ C \) \( N_2, O_2, CO_2 \), water vapor
Stratosphere 18–50 Contains the ozone layer (20–40 km) which absorbs ultraviolet radiation. Temperature inversion occurs. \( -50^\circ C \) to \( 10^\circ C \) \( N_2, O_2, O_3 \), O-atoms
Mesosphere 50–85 Temperature decreases with height; meteors burn up in this layer. \( -90^\circ C \) to \( -100^\circ C \) \( N_2, O_2, O \), \( NO^+ \)
Thermosphere 85–640 High temperatures due to low pressure; International Space Station orbits here. Up to \( 1500^\circ C \) \( O^+, O_2^+, NO^+ \)
Exosphere 500–1600 Highest region with ionized gases. Varies Ionized gases
- Ionosphere: - Description: - A region from 85–600 km, including parts of the thermosphere, mesosphere, and exosphere, ionized by solar radiation. - Ozone Types: - Good Ozone: - Found in the stratosphere, formed by oxygen molecules (\( O_2 \)). - Beneficial for protecting living organisms from ultraviolet radiation. - Bad Ozone: - Found in the troposphere, formed by reactions of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) and nitrogen dioxide (\( NO_2 \)). - Harmful to living organisms. - Pollution - Definition: - The presence of abnormally high concentrations of harmful substances in the environment, causing undesirable changes in the physical, chemical, and biological properties of air, water, and soil. - Affects human beings directly or indirectly. - Causes: - Continuous rise in human population is a major contributor to pollution. - Pollutants - Definition: - Substances (chemical or other factors) that cause pollution and disrupt the natural balance of the environment. - Can be natural or man-made in origin. - Types of Pollutants Based on Production: - Primary Pollutants: - Directly emitted into the atmosphere. - Examples: - Ash from volcanic eruptions - Carbon monoxide (\( CO \)) from vehicle exhausts - Sulphur dioxide (\( SO_2 \)) from factories - Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs) - Carbon dioxide (\( CO_2 \)) from combustion - Suspended particulate matter (smoke, dust, vapor) - Nitrogen oxides (\( NO_x \)) from fuel burning - Secondary Pollutants: - Formed when primary pollutants react in the atmosphere. - Examples: - Peroxyacyl Nitrate (PAN) - Ground-level ozone formed from \( NO_x \) and VOCs - Priority of Pollutants (UNEP Classification): -
Order of Priority Pollutant Medium
1 Sulphur dioxide and suspended particles Air
2 Strontium, caesium Food
3 Ozone Air
4 DDT and other organochlorine compounds Biota, man
5 Nitrates and nitrites Drinking water
6 Nitrogen oxides Air
7 Mercury compounds Food, water
8 Lead and cobalt Food, air
9 Petroleum hydrocarbons Sea
10 Carbon monoxide Air
11 Fluorides Fresh water
12 Asbestos Air
13 Arsenic Drinking water
14 Mycotoxins and microbial contaminants Food
- Relative Contribution to Pollution: - Order: - \( CO > SO_2 > \text{hydrocarbons} > \text{particulates} > \text{nitrogen oxides} \) - Types of Pollutants Based on Decomposition: - Biodegradable Pollutants: - Degraded by natural means (e.g., bacteria, fungi). - Examples: - Municipal solid waste (green waste), food waste, paper waste, biodegradable plastics, human waste, manure, sewage, slaughterhouse waste. - Note: - Most biodegradable wastes are converted to methane (\( CH_4 \)) via anaerobic digestion (without oxygen). - Non-biodegradable Pollutants: - Do not degrade or degrade very slowly in nature, often toxic and entering food chains. - Examples: - Heavy metal salts, radioactive materials, DDT, long-chain phenolic chemicals. - Synergism: - Phenomenon where pollutants interact to increase toxicity. - Types of Pollution - Air Pollution: - Definition: - The occurrence or addition of foreign particles or high concentrations of gases that disrupt the natural ratio of air components. - Sources of Air Pollution: -
Source Pollutants Origin
Photochemical Products PAN, PBN, Benzpyrene Photolysis of \( NO_x, O_3 \), hydrocarbons
Oxides of Nitrogen \( NO_x \) Automobile exhaust, chemical industries
Carbon Monoxide \( CO \) Automobile exhaust
Toxicants and Heavy Metals \( Hg, Ni, Pb, Cd \) Metallurgical operations
Smoke Particulate matter Incomplete combustion, thermal power plants
Sulphur Dioxide \( SO_2 \) Combustion of fossil fuels, smelting
Hydrogen Sulphide \( H_2S \) Industrial effluent
Hydrogen Fluoride \( HF \) Laundry, fertilizers, aluminum smelting
Aldehydes and Organic Acids Organic compounds Incomplete combustion of petroleum
Fume Metal particles, oxides Condensation of vapor, sublimation
Dust (SPM) Wood dust, sand Mechanical disintegration (e.g., woodworking, sandblasting)
Secondary Pollutants \( NO_2, O_3, N_2O_5, COOH, RCHO \) Photochemical reactions of primary pollutants
- Effects of Air Pollution: - On Human Health: - Carbon Monoxide (\( CO \)): - Competes with oxygen for hemoglobin, forming carboxyhemoglobin (\( COHb \)), which is highly poisonous. - Symptoms: - Headache, irritation, decreased vision, cardiovascular malfunction, asphyxia. - Particulate Matter: - Includes soot, fly ash, dust, fur, hair, spores, pollen, heavy metals. - Causes respiratory problems. - Note: - Respirable Suspended Particulate Matter (RSPM) has a diameter < 10 µm. - Carbon Dioxide (\( CO_2 \)): - Greenhouse gas with rising concentrations. - Excess causes headache and nausea. - Hydrogen Sulphide (\( H_2S \)): - Byproduct of metallurgical treatment of sulfur-containing ores. - Causes chlorosis and defoliation in plants, eye/throat irritation, and nausea in animals. - Sulphur Oxides (\( SO_x \)): - Primarily \( SO_2 \), produced during smelting and burning of fossil fuels. - Causes respiratory problems, including permanent lung damage. - On Plants: - Affected by pollutants like \( H_2S \) and \( SO_2 \), leading to chlorosis and defoliation. - Smog: - Definition: - A combination of smoke and fog, categorized into two types. - Classical Smog: - Occurs in cool, humid climates. - Mixture of smoke, fog, and \( SO_2 \). - Known as reducing smog due to its chemical nature. - Photochemical Smog: - Occurs in warm, dry, sunny climates. - Consists of nitrogen dioxide (\( NO_2 \)), hydrocarbons, ozone (\( O_3 \)), and Peroxyacetyl Nitrate (PAN). - Known as oxidizing smog. - Formation Reactions: - \( NO + \text{sunlight} \rightarrow NO_2 \) - \( NO_2 + \text{sunlight} \rightarrow NO + O \) - \( O + O_2 \rightarrow O_3 \) - \( O_3 + \text{hydrocarbons} \rightarrow \text{formaldehyde, acrolein, PAN} \) - Effects: - Ozone and \( NO_2 \) cause headache, chest pain, throat dryness, cough, and breathing difficulties. - Causes cracking of rubber, corrosion of metals, and damage to building materials. - Control Measures: - Use of catalytic converters in vehicles to reduce \( NO_x \) and hydrocarbon emissions. - Plants like Pinus, Pyrus, Juniperus, and Vitis metabolize nitrogen oxides. - Bhopal Gas Tragedy: - Date: - December 2, 1984 - Location: - Bhopal, India - Cause: - Release of methyl isocyanate (MIC) gas from a Union Carbide pesticide plant. - Impact: - Approximately 3,200 deaths, lung diseases, eye irritation, and blindness. - Control of Air Pollution: - Methods: - Separating pollutants from harmless gases. - Converting pollutants to non-toxic products before release. - Using suitable fuels and efficient engines. - Selecting appropriate locations for manufacturing sites. - Raising social awareness. - Legislation: - Air Prevention and Control of Pollution Act, 1981: - Enacted to prevent, control, and abate air pollution. - Amended in 1987 to include noise as an air pollutant. - Declared the National Capital Territory of Delhi as an air pollution control area, specifying approved fuels for vehicles. - Vehicular Pollution Control in Delhi: - Delhi was the most polluted city in 1990. - A Public Interest Litigation (PIL) led to the conversion of public transport (e.g., buses) to Compressed Natural Gas (CNG). - Advantages of CNG: - Burns efficiently with no unburnt residue. - Cheaper than petrol, cannot be siphoned or adulterated. - Water Pollution: - Definition: - Degradation of water quality due to the addition of substances (inorganic, organic, biological, radiological) or factors (e.g., heat) that make it unfit for human use or aquatic life. - WHO Definition: - Occurs when foreign materials are added to water supplies, causing toxicity, reduced oxygen levels, aesthetically unsuitable effects, or epidemic diseases. - Potable Water: - Definition: - Water suitable for drinking. - Methods to Make Water Potable: - Filtration (e.g., candle-type filters). - Boiling to kill germs. - Chlorination using chlorine tablets or bleaching powder. - Sources of Water Pollution: -
Source Pollutants Origin
Industrial Agents Free chlorine, acids, fats, oils, grease, starch, mineral acids, fluorides, cyanogen, limestone, hydrocarbons, phenols, sulphides, chromium, tannic acid, lead Paper/pulp, textile, food processing, chemical, metal, petroleum, tanneries, battery, wool scouring industries
Fertilizers and Farm Wastes Runoff from agricultural fields Agriculture
Synthetic Detergents Detergents Washing clothes near water bodies
Pesticides DDT, 2,4-D, TEPP, Aldrin, BHC, Parathion Agricultural use
Household Wastage and Sewage Carbohydrates, proteins, sugars, starch, cellulose, dextrin, glycogen, alginic acid Domestic waste
Toxic Metals \( Pb, Zn, Ar, Cu, Cd, Hg, Ni \) Electroplating, chemical, copper pickling industries
Acids \( HNO_3, H_2SO_4 \) Chemical industries
Dyes Chemical dyes Chemical industries
Fats, Soaps, Waxes Fats, soaps, waxes Food and household industries
Gaseous Pollutants \( NH_3, Cl, H_2S, O_2, \text{Phosphine} \) Industrial waste
Oils Hydrocarbons Tanks, machines, lubricants, refineries
- Types of Water Pollutants: - Point Source: - Pollutants entering from a single, identifiable source. - Non-point Source: - Pollutants entering from diffuse, widespread sources. - Effects of Water Pollution: - Eutrophication: - Natural aging of lakes or water bodies due to excessive nutrients (e.g., nitrogen, phosphorus). - Symptoms: - Hypoxia (oxygen depletion) - Harmful algal blooms - Consequences: - Algal blooms consume oxygen, reducing availability for aquatic life. - Blocks sunlight, hindering photosynthesis in marine plants. - Some algae produce toxins harmful to higher life forms. - Example: - Water hyacinth (\( Eichhornia \, crassipes \)), known as the "Terror of Bengal," grows excessively in eutrophic lakes, disrupting ecosystem dynamics. - Biomagnification: - Increase in toxicant concentration (e.g., DDT, mercury) at successive trophic levels. - Mechanism: - Substances that cannot be metabolized or excreted accumulate in organisms and pass through the food chain. - Example: - High DDT concentrations interfere with calcium metabolism in birds, causing eggshell thinning and population decline. - Effects on Human Health: - Fluoride Deficiency: - Causes tooth decay. - Fluoride Excess (>2 ppm): - Brown mottling of teeth; >10 ppm harms bones and teeth, causing skeletal fluorosis. - Lead (>50 ppm): - Damages kidneys, liver, reproductive system. - Sulphate (>500 ppm): - Laxative effect. - Cadmium: - Causes Itai-Itai disease (bone softening), observed in Japan. - Mercury: - Causes Minamata disease, first observed in Minamata Bay, Japan (1953), leading to nervous damage and deaths. - Nitrate (>50 ppm): - Causes Blue-baby syndrome (Methaemoglobinemia), inhibiting oxygen transport. - Contaminated Water: - Spreads diseases like cholera, typhoid, hepatitis, and E. coli infections. - Measurement of Water Pollution: - Dissolved Oxygen (DO): - Essential for aquatic life; optimal value is 5–6 ppm. - Below 5 ppm inhibits fish growth. - Excess organic matter leads to anaerobic decomposition, producing foul odors. - Biochemical Oxygen Demand (BOD): - Amount of oxygen used by microorganisms for biochemical oxidation in a unit volume of water. - Indicates the degree of organic pollution. - Influenced by microorganism type, pH, toxins, reduced minerals, and nitrification. - Chemical Oxygen Demand (COD): - Amount of oxygen required to oxidize organic matter using a strong chemical oxidant (e.g., potassium dichromate) in 2 hours. - Less accurate for organic matter strength, as it also oxidizes inorganic matter (e.g., nitrates, sulphates). - Unaffected by toxins or conditions inhibiting microbial growth. - Control Measures for Water Pollution: - Integrated Wastewater Treatment: - Process: - Removal of large objects via bar screens. - Grit and sand removal in tanks. - Settling of solids (sludge) in large tanks; skimming of floating solids (e.g., oil, grease). - Aerobic bacteria decompose remaining waste, forming activated sludge. - Treated water discharged into seas or rivers. - Legislation: - Water Prevention and Control of Pollution Act, 1974: - Enacted to prevent and control water pollution and restore water quality. - Amended in 1988. - Water Prevention and Control of Pollution Cess Act, 1977: - Levies a cess on water used by industries to fund pollution control boards. - Amended in 2003. - Other Methods: - Recycling industrial effluents to prevent pollutant release. - Removing pollutants via absorption, electrodialysis, ion-exchange, reverse osmosis, and biotechnology. - Ecological Sanitation (Ecosan): - Sustainable system using composite toilets for human excreta disposal. - Advantages: - Hygienic, efficient, and cost-effective. - Recycles excreta into natural fertilizer, replacing chemical fertilizers. - Implementation: - Common in Kerala and Sri Lanka. - Ganga Action Plan (GAP): - Launched in April 1985 to reduce pollution in the Ganga River. - Withdrawn on March 31, 2000, due to limited success. - Yamuna Action Plan (YAP): - Bilateral project between India and Japan, funded by the Japanese Bank for International Cooperation (17.7 billion yen). - Launched in 1993, extended to February 2003. - Aims to restore the Yamuna River. - Bioremediation: - Uses biological agents (e.g., bacteria, fungi) to break down pollutants into less toxic or non-toxic substances. - Types: - In situ: - Treating contamination at the site. - Ex situ: - Treating contamination elsewhere. - Technologies: - Phytoremediation, bioleaching, landfarming, bioreactor, composting, biostimulation. - Advantages: - Safe and less expensive than incineration or landfilling. - Treats contamination in place, avoiding excavation. - Example: Oil Zapper: - Developed by TERI for cleaning oil spills using bacteria. - Used in Mumbai (August 2010) after a 400-tonne oil spill. - Mixture of five bacterial strains and corncob powder converts hydrocarbons to \( CO_2 \) and water. - Soil and Land Pollution: - Definition: - Alteration of soil due to pollutants, reducing productivity, plant quantity, and groundwater quality. - Caused by solid and semi-solid substances (e.g., pesticides, fertilizers) from agricultural practices and insanitary habits. - Sources of Soil Pollution: -
Source Pollutants Details
Biological Agents Excreta, digested sewage sludge, pathogenic bacteria, parasitic worms From humans, animals (e.g., cows, pigs, sheep), or soil due to edaphic causes. Causes diseases like dysentery, cholera, typhoid.
Radiological Agents Ruthenium-106, Rhodium-106, Iodine-131, Barium-140, Lanthanum-140, Cerium-144, Promethium-144, Carbon-14, Cesium-137 From nuclear explosions, radioactive wastes, and testing. Causes health hazards like cancer.
Industrial and Urban Waste Coal/mineral wastes, sludge, garbage, rubbish (paper, fuel residues, glass, rubble, abandoned vehicles) From mining, metal processing, engineering industries, and domestic/community waste. Causes aesthetic and health issues (e.g., dysentery, diarrhea).
Agricultural Practices Fertilizers, pesticides (DDT, BHC, Endrin, Aldrin, Dieldrin), organosulfurous compounds, Pb, Hg, Ar Phosphates, nitrates, and toxic chemicals harm plants and promote pest proliferation.
- Effects of Soil Pollution: - Reduced soil fertility and loss of organism diversity. - Soil erosion due to removal of topsoil by wind or water. - Death of non-target organisms. - Biomagnification of pesticides. - Control Measures: - Minimizing pesticide use. - Adopting proper waste dumping techniques. - E-Waste (Electronic Waste): - Definition: - Wastes from used electronic devices and appliances unsuitable for their original purpose, suited for recovery, recycling, or disposal. - Examples: - Computers, mobile phones, stereos, refrigerators, air conditioners. - Treatment: - Buried in landfills or incinerated. - Exported to developing countries (e.g., China, India, Pakistan) for metal recovery (copper, iron, silicon, nickel, gold). - Manual recycling in developing countries exposes workers to toxic substances. - Developed countries have specialized recycling facilities. - Sound or Noise Pollution: - Definition: - Presence of disturbing, unpleasant sound that harms human or animal life. - Sources: - Indoor: - Machines, building activities, music. - Outdoor: - Transportation systems, motor vehicles, aircraft. - Causes: - Vehicle noise, construction machinery, industrial machines, music concerts. - Effects: - Hearing impairment, hypertension, ischemic heart disease, annoyance, sleep disturbance. - Immune system changes, birth defects, tinnitus, vasoconstriction, cardiovascular issues. - Psychological effects: - Ill-temperedness, anger, frustration, increased heart rate, irritation, adrenal hormone secretion. - Control Measures: - Planting trees to suppress noise. - Creating horn-free zones near hospitals and schools. - Enforcing time restrictions for loudspeakers. - Using silencers in automobiles and industrial equipment. - Adhering to permissible sound levels for firecrackers and loudspeakers. - Using sound-absorbent materials in buildings. - Note: The Air Prevention and Control of Pollution Act, 1981, amended in 1987, includes noise as an air pollutant. - Radioactive Pollution: - Definition: - Pollution of air, water, and soil with radioactive materials emitting particles (\( \alpha, \beta, \gamma \)). - Examples: - Radium, thorium, uranium. - Causes: - Man-made radiations from human activities. - Sources: - Medical wastes, coal ash, atomic weapons/explosions, nuclear reactors/fuel, mining, leakage from power plants, unsafe disposal of radioactive waste. - Effects: - Mutates DNA, causing abnormal growth and cancer. - Radiation persists in the atmosphere for years. - Climate - Definition: - Long-term (at least 30 years) characteristic condition of the atmosphere near Earth’s surface, including weather patterns, seasons, and extremes. - Determines the flora and fauna of a region. - Climate Change: - Significant, lasting changes in weather patterns over decades to millions of years. - Causes: - Human: - Increased greenhouse gas emissions, deforestation. - Natural: - Variations in solar radiation, volcanic activity. - Concern: - Human activities have accelerated climate change. - Greenhouse Effect - Definition: - Process where thermal radiation from Earth’s surface is absorbed by greenhouse gases and re-radiated, elevating surface temperatures. - Greenhouse Gases: -
Gas Atmospheric Concentration Atmospheric Lifetime Sources Effects
Carbon Dioxide (\( CO_2 \)) 380 ppm 5–200 years Fossil fuel burning, deforestation Increases photosynthesis, reduces stomatal conductance and transpiration
Nitrous Oxide (\( N_2O \)) 316 ppb Varies Agriculture, biomass burning, nylon industries, nitrogen-rich fertilizers Contributes to greenhouse effect
Methane (\( CH_4 \)) 1750 ppb Varies Freshwater wetlands, cattle, rice fields, biomass burning Produced by methanogen bacteria, enhances greenhouse effect
Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs) 282 ppt 45–260 years Air conditioners, refrigeration, solvents, plastic foams, aerosol propellants Contributes to ozone depletion and greenhouse effect
- Global Warming - Definition: - Increase in Earth’s average atmospheric and oceanic temperatures due to rising \( CO_2 \) concentrations since the late 19th century. - Causes: - Burning fossil fuels (coal, oil, natural gas). - Increasing greenhouse gases. - Deforestation for pastures and plantations. - Carbon accumulation. - Effects: - Temperature increase of \( 0.6^\circ C \) over the last three decades. - Changes in precipitation patterns. - Deleterious environmental changes, e.g., El Niño effect. - Example: - Gangotri glacier in the Himalayas melting due to global warming. - El Niño: - Abnormal warming of Eastern Pacific Ocean surface waters. - Effects: - Coral reef bleaching and death. - Altered migration routes for birds, fish, and whales. - Changes in rainfall patterns and drought. - Contributes to biodiversity loss and rising sea levels due to melting polar ice caps. - International Efforts: - Kyoto Protocol: - International agreement to reduce \( CO_2 \) and greenhouse gas emissions. - Acid Rain - Definition: - Rain with pH < 5, compared to normal rain (pH 5.6–6.5). - Caused by atmospheric pollutants like \( NO_x \), \( SO_2 \), and VOCs. - Chemical Reactions: - \( 2SO_2 + O_2 \rightarrow 2SO_3 \) - \( SO_3 + H_2O \rightarrow H_2SO_4 \) - \( 2NO_2 + [O] \rightarrow N_2O_5 \) - \( N_2O_5 + H_2O \rightarrow 2HNO_3 \) - Effects: - Damages monuments, e.g., Taj Mahal due to reaction with marble (\( CaCO_3 \)). - Causes "stone cancer" from acid rain exposure. - Example: - Most acidic rain recorded in West Virginia, USA, with pH 1.5. - Stratospheric Pollution (Ozone Depletion) - Definition: - Reduction in stratospheric ozone (\( O_3 \)) that protects against harmful UV-B radiation (280–315 nm). - Ozone Formation: - \( O_2 \xrightarrow{\text{UV}} O + O \) - \( O + O_2 \rightarrow O_3 \) - Causes of Depletion: - Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs) release chlorine free radicals under UV radiation, which react with \( O_3 \). - Example Reaction: - \( CFC \xrightarrow{\text{UV}} Cl^\cdot + \text{other products} \) - \( Cl^\cdot + O_3 \rightarrow ClO + O_2 \) - Other Ozone-Depleting Substances (ODS) contribute similarly. - Ozone Hole: - Significant depletion over Antarctica, forming in September–October and replenished after spring. - Caused by polar vortex preventing ozone-rich air from filling the gap. - Impacts of Ozone Depletion: - On Humans: - Increased skin cancers, sunburns, premature skin aging. - Cataracts, damaging the lens, cornea, retina, and conjunctiva. - Weakened immune system, increasing skin cancer risk. - On Flora: - Reduced growth, photosynthesis, and flowering in crops (e.g., wheat, rice, corn, soybeans). - Harm to tree seedlings. - On Animals: - Eye and skin cancers in domestic animals. - Threatens marine species in developmental stages (e.g., fish, shrimp, crab larvae). - On Aquatic/Marine Life: - Reduced plankton populations disrupt food chains, leading to species shifts and reduced fish yields. - On Materials: - Degradation of wood, plastic, rubber, fabrics, and construction materials. - International Efforts: - Montreal Protocol (1987): - Over 150 countries agreed to phase out CFC production. - Complete ban on CFCs effective from January 1996. - Chlorine accumulation expected to persist for a decade. - Ozone levels in Antarctica may return to 1970s levels by mid-21st century. - World Ozone Day: - September 16, designated by the UN General Assembly. - Environmental Health - Definition (WHO): - Aspects of human health and disease determined by environmental factors. - Involves assessing and controlling environmental factors that affect health. - Disciplines: - Environmental epidemiology, toxicology, exposure science. - Overlap: - Each discipline provides unique insights but overlaps in addressing environmental health issues. - Green Chemistry - Definition: - Utilizes chemistry and other sciences to reduce adverse environmental impacts. - Applications in Daily Life: - Dry Cleaning: - Replaced tetra-chloroethene (a suspected carcinogen) with liquefied \( CO_2 \) and suitable detergents, less harmful to groundwater. - Paper Bleaching: - Uses hydrogen peroxide (\( H_2O_2 \)) with catalysts instead of chlorine. - Chemical Synthesis: - Ethanol produced by one-step oxidation of ethene with an ionic catalyst, yielding 90%. - Ecology and Ecosystem - Ecology - Definition: - Ecology (from Greek: *Oikas* = living place, *logos* = study) is the science that studies organisms and their relationships with their environment. - Haeckel defined ecology as the branch of biology dealing with organisms and their mutual relationships with their surroundings (atmosphere). - Types of Ecology: - Autoecology: - Description: - Focuses on the ecology of a single species, examining the relationship between that species and its environment. - Synecology: - Description: - Studies the inter-relationships among communities of organisms in a specific region. - Categories: - Population Ecology: - Involves understanding, explaining, and predicting species distributions and how species populations interact with the environment. - Community Ecology: - Examines interactions between species in communities, including their distribution, structure, and abundance. - A community is an assemblage of populations of two or more different species occupying the same geographical area. - Ecosystemology: - Combined study of biotic and abiotic components of an ecosystem and their interactions within an ecosystem framework. - Ecological Notes: - Feeding Habits: - Some animals are specialists (e.g., Koala bear, feeding on specific food like eucalyptus leaves), while others are generalists, consuming a wide variety of food. - Specialists are efficient but face a higher extinction risk if their food source is disrupted. - Habitat vs. Niche: - Habitat is the "address" of a species (where it lives). - Niche is the "profession" of a species (its ecological role and interactions). - Important Ecological Terms - Definitions: -
Term Definition
Population Group of individuals of a particular species in a specific area.
Population Types Expanding (increasing), stable, or declining (decreasing).
Population Density Number of individuals of a population in a given area.
Population Growth Increase in the size of a population over time.
Natality (Birth Rate) Number of births during a given period in a population.
Mortality (Death Rate) Number of deaths during a given period in a population.
Immigration Number of individuals entering a population.
Emigration Number of individuals leaving a population.
Sex Ratio Comparative percentage of males and females in a population.
Gross Productivity Rate of production of organic matter during photosynthesis by producers.
Net Primary Productivity Gross primary productivity minus respiration losses.
Standing Crop Mass of living organisms (biomass) or number in a unit area.
Population Interaction Interactions between members of the same or different species, which may be beneficial (+) or harmful (-) for one or both parties.
Age Pyramids Vertical bar graph representing the number of individuals of various ages at a given time.
Niche Ecological role and space occupied by an organism in a community.
Ectotherms (Conformers) Animals whose body temperature changes with the surrounding environment.
Endotherms (Regulators) Animals that maintain a constant body temperature.
Aestivation Summer sleep, where animals enter a dormant state during hot periods.
Hibernation Winter sleep, where animals enter a dormant state during cold periods.
- Ecosystem - Definition: - Coined by A.G. Tansley in 1935, an ecosystem is a functional unit comprising all living beings in an area and their non-living environmental components, interacting to form a stable system. - Types of Ecosystems: - Natural Ecosystems: - Examples: Forests, deserts, grasslands (terrestrial); ponds, lakes, wetlands (aquatic). - Artificial Ecosystems: - Examples: - Gardens, aquariums, crop fields (man-made). - Components of an Ecosystem: - Abiotic Components (Non-living Factors): - Temperature: - Directly affects organism physiology and activities (e.g., reproduction). - Optimal range for biotic activity: - \( 10^\circ C \) to \( 45^\circ C \). - Thermoperiodism: - Temperature-dependent activities in plants (e.g., evaporation increases with temperature). - Light: - Essential for photosynthesis, enabling plants to produce food. - Affects locomotion, seed sprouting, respiration, and evaporation. - Wind: - Influences soil erosion, plant binding, pollination, and seed dispersal. - Humidity: - Inversely related to evaporation (low humidity increases evaporation). - Topographic Factors: - Includes land expansion, mountains, altitude, latitude, affecting organism distribution. - Edaphic Factors: - Related to soil properties (structure, wind, water) that interact with vegetation. - Availability of Food: - Organisms are more abundant where food is readily available. - Biotic Components (Living Organisms): - Producers (Autotrophs): - Organisms that synthesize food from inorganic substances (e.g., \( CO_2 \), water) using sunlight and chlorophyll. - Biomass: - Organic material produced by plants. - Primary Productivity: Rate at which producers form biomass. - Examples: - Green plants, blue-green algae. - Consumers (Heterotrophs): - Organisms that feed on other organisms or their parts. - Types: - Primary Consumers (Herbivores): - Feed on producers. - Examples: - Goats, deer, cows. - Secondary Consumers (Carnivores): - Feed on primary consumers. - Examples: - Wolves, snakes, humans. - Tertiary Consumers (Carnivores): - Feed on secondary consumers. - Examples: - Lions, hawks, tigers. - Omnivores: - Consume both producers and consumers. - Examples: - Humans, crows. - Detritivores: - Feed on detritus (dead organisms or organic waste). - Examples: - Crows, vultures. - Decomposers (Reducers): - Feed on dead organisms and organic waste, breaking them down. - Examples: - Termites, beetles, bacteria, saprophytic fungi (e.g., mushrooms). - Functions of an Ecosystem: - Material or nutrient cycling. - Biological or ecological regulation: - Regulation of organisms by the environment. - Regulation of the environment by organisms. - Energy Flow in Ecosystem - - Description: - Energy flow is unidirectional, from the sun to producers, then to consumers, adhering to the first law of thermodynamics. - Energy is transferred as food, converted into energy during metabolism, and stored as biomass. - Food Chain: - Definition: - Sequence of "who eats whom" in a biological community to obtain nutrients, starting with the sun (primary energy source) to producers and consumers. - Characteristics: - Ends with a top predator (no natural enemies). - Energy flow is unidirectional, unavailable to previous trophic levels. - Example: - \( \text{Grass} \rightarrow \text{Insect} \rightarrow \text{Frog} \rightarrow \text{Snake} \rightarrow \text{Hawk} \) - Grass: - Primary producer. - Insect: - Primary consumer. - Frog: - Secondary consumer. - Snake: - Secondary carnivore. - Hawk: - Tertiary carnivore. - Decomposers (bacteria, fungi) break down remains. - Note: - Except in deep-sea hydrothermal ecosystems, all food chains start with photosynthesis and end with decay. - Energy is lost at each trophic level. - Ten Percent Law (Lindeman’s Rule): - Proposed by Lindeman. - Only about 10% of energy is transferred from one trophic level to the next. - Remaining energy is lost during transfer, respiration, or incomplete digestion. - Food Web: - Definition: - A network of interconnected food chains showing feeding relationships and energy/nutrient transfer among species. - Representation: - Graphical depiction of energy and material flow (e.g., carbon) in a community. - Examples: - Forest Ecosystem Food Chain. - Grassland Ecosystem Food Chain. - Aquatic Ecosystem Food Chain. - Trophic Levels: - - Definition: - Specific positions in a food chain based on feeding relationships. - Structure: - First trophic level: Producers (green plants). - Second trophic level: Herbivores (primary consumers). - Third/Fourth trophic levels: Carnivores and omnivores (secondary/tertiary consumers). - Note: - Classification is based on function, not population. - Ecological Pyramids - Definition: - Graphical representation of relationships between trophic levels in terms of number, biomass, or energy. - Structure: - Base: - Producers (first trophic level). - Apex: - Top-level consumers (tertiary consumers). - Generally upright, with few exceptions. - Types: - Pyramid of Number: - Represents the total number of organisms at each trophic level. - Pyramid of Biomass: - Represents the total dry mass of organisms at each trophic level. - Pyramid of Energy: - Represents the total energy of organisms at each trophic level. - Always upright. - Ecological Succession - Definition: - Process by which an ecological community undergoes orderly, predictable changes following a disturbance or initial colonization of a new habitat. - First studied by King (1685) and George-Buffon (1742). - Causes: - Initiating causes (e.g., disturbances like fire or colonization). - Ecesic causes (environmental changes driven by organisms). - Stabilizing causes (processes that maintain community stability). - Types: - Primary Succession: - Occurs in areas with no prior living organisms (e.g., sandy land, bare rocks). - Secondary Succession: - Occurs in areas where an ecological community previously existed but was destroyed (e.g., forest regrowth after a fire). - Ecological Adaptations in Plants - Definition: - Special features enabling plants to survive and grow in specific habitats. - Types of Plants Based on Habitat: - Hydrophytes: - Grow in water-rich environments. - Types: - Free-floating: - *Eichhornia crassipes*, *Lemna minor*. - Submerged: - *Hydrilla verticillata*, *Ceratophyllum*. - Attached-floating: - *Nelumbium speciosum*, *Nelumbo nucifera*. - Attached-emergent: - *Typha*, *Cyperus*. - Marshy: - *Jussiaea repens*, *Dentella repens*. - Adaptations: - Roots absent or poorly developed (e.g., *Wolffia*, *Salvinia*). - Well-developed roots with root caps and aerenchyma in emergent hydrophytes (e.g., *Ranunculus*). - Root hairs absent or poorly developed. - Root caps replaced by root pockets in *Eichhornia*. - Stems long, slender, spongy, flexible (e.g., *Hydrilla*). - Leaves thin, ribbon-shaped, or finely dissected in submerged forms (e.g., *Vallisneria*, *Ceratophyllum*). - Floating leaves large, flat, entire, wax-coated (e.g., *Nymphaea*). - Heterophylly in *Ranunculus*, *Sagittaria*. - Mesophytes: - Grow in areas with moderate water supply. - Examples: - Garden plants, crops. - Xerophytes: - Grow in water-scarce environments (e.g., deserts), adapted to reduce transpiration. - Examples: - *Agave*, *Begonia*, *Aloe*, cacti. - Adaptations: - Extensive, long tap roots with widespread branching. - Well-developed root hairs and root caps. - Stems stunted, woody, dry, hard, with thick bark. - Stems green and fleshy in *Opuntia* (phylloclade). - Hairs and waxy coatings on stems/leaves. - Leaves reduced or modified into spines. - Leaf surfaces shiny/glazed to reflect light and heat. - Leaves folded/rolled to reduce transpiration (e.g., *Ammophila*, *Poa*, *Agropyron*). - Stipules modified into spines (e.g., *Euphorbia*, *Acacia*, *Capparis*). - Sunken stomata. - Presence of oils, resins, prolines, and chaperonins (heat-shock proteins) in non-succulents (e.g., *Prosopis*, *Calotropis*). - Epiphytes: - Grow on other plants without harming them, photo-autotrophic. - Examples: - Orchids. - Halophytes: - Tolerate saline environments (3–5% salinity). - Examples: - Mangroves. - Marshy Plants: - Grow in both aquatic and terrestrial environments. - Examples: - White water lilies. - Oxalophytes: - Thrive in acidic environments. - Examples: - *Saxifraga oppositifolia*. - Eremophytes: - Desert plants. - Examples: - Cacti. - Helophytes: - Grow in marshy or lake-edge environments with perennating organs in soil/mud below water, but aerial shoots above water. - Examples: - *Phragmites*. - Psammophytes: - Grow in sandy soils, often stabilizing them. - Examples: - *Haloxylon persicum*. - Ecological Relationships - Definition: - Interactions between organisms in an ecosystem, influenced by prior interactions. - Types: - Oppositional Relationships: - Predation: - Interaction between predator (hunter) and prey (hunted). - Competition: - Organisms compete for resources (food, space, shelter, mates, ecological status). - Symbiotic Relationships: - Parasitism: - Non-functional relationship where one organism (parasite) benefits at the expense of another (host). - Commensalism: - One organism benefits without affecting the other. - Mutualism: - Both organisms derive fitness benefits. - Saprophytism: - Organisms (e.g., bacteria, Protozoa) grow on dead, decaying organic matter. - Amensalism: - Product of one organism negatively affects another. - Epiphyte: - Plant grows on another for support, not food. - Nutrient Flow in Ecosystem - Definition: - Nutrient cycle (biogeochemical cycle) involves the exchange/circulation of biogenetic nutrients between living and non-living components of the biosphere. - Biogenetic Nutrients: - Essential elements for organism growth and metabolism. - Types of Biogeochemical Cycles: - Gaseous Cycles: - Carbon Cycle: - - Carbon exchange among biosphere, pedosphere, geosphere, and atmosphere. - Discovered by Joseph Priestley and Antoine Lavoisier. - Process: - \( CO_2 \) in atmosphere used in photosynthesis (terrestrial/aquatic food chains). - Respiration and decomposition release \( CO_2 \). - Burning of forests, fuelwood, fossil fuels (vehicles, electricity, heat) releases \( CO_2 \). - Organic sediments form coal, oil, gas, limestone, dolomite. - Nitrogen Cycle: - - Involves fixation, mineralization, nitrification, and denitrification. - Process: - Aerial \( N_2 \) fixed by bacteria or lightning into nitrates (\( NO_3^- \)). - Plants convert nitrates to organic nitrogen. - Animals consume plants, forming organic animal nitrogen. - Bacteria convert nitrites (\( NO_2^- \)) and ammonia (\( NH_2 \)) back to nitrates. - Denitrifying bacteria release \( N_2 \). - Fertilizers and rock dissolution contribute nitrates. - Oxygen Cycle: - - Movement of oxygen through atmosphere, biosphere, and lithosphere. - Process: - Photosynthesis produces oxygen (\( O_2 \)) from \( CO_2 \) and \( H_2O \). - Respiration consumes \( O_2 \), releasing \( CO_2 \). - Detritus food chains and decay involve oxygen. - Sedimentary Cycles: - Phosphorus Cycle: - - Movement of phosphorus through lithosphere, hydrosphere, and biosphere. - Atmosphere plays a minor role. - Process: - Inorganic phosphorus from natural/human sources taken up by plants, converted to organic phosphorus. - Animals consume plants, incorporating organic phosphorus. - Death, excretion, and decomposition return inorganic phosphorus to the water column or soil via bacterial action. - Sulphur Cycle: - Sulphur, a secondary element with calcium and magnesium, is found in soil as organic matter. - Essential for proteins and co-factors. - Process: - Mineralization converts organic sulphur to hydrogen sulphide (\( H_2S \)). - Oxidation converts \( H_2S \) to sulphate (\( SO_4^{2-} \)). - Reduction converts sulphate to sulphide. - Sulphide incorporated into organic compounds. - Water Cycle (Hydrological Cycle): - Description: - Continuous movement of water (\( H_2O \)) on, above, and below Earth’s surface through evaporation, condensation, and precipitation. - Nutrients dissolved in water circulate within the cycle. - Influences: - Affects climate and temperature changes. - Human Impacts: - Agriculture, industry, dams, urbanization, and groundwater use alter the cycle. - Biosphere - Definition: - The sum of all ecosystems, termed the "zone of life" on Earth. - Acts as a closed, largely self-regulating system. - Integrates all living beings and their relationships. - Evolution: - Originated through biopoesis (life from non-living matter) or biogenesis (life from living matter). - Biomes - - Definition: - Geographical areas classified by the plants and animals living there, distinct from ecosystems (interactions of living/non-living components). - A biome may contain multiple ecosystems (e.g., coral reefs, kelp forests in an aquatic biome). - Types: - Terrestrial Biomes: - Forest Ecosystems: - Boreal Forest - Temperate Deciduous Forest - Tropical Rain Forest - Grassland Ecosystems: - Temperate Grassland - Tropical Grassland - Desert Ecosystems: - Cold Desert - Hot Desert - Aquatic Biomes: - Freshwater - Marine Water - Biodiversity - Definition: - Variety of life forms and ecosystems on Earth, including terrestrial, marine, and aquatic ecosystems. - Levels of Biodiversity: - Genetic Biodiversity: - Heritable variation within and between populations. - Species Biodiversity: - Species richness (number of species in a site or habitat). - Ecosystem/Community Biodiversity: - Diversity at ecosystem or community levels, challenging to assess globally due to lack of unique definitions. - Mapping Species Biodiversity: - First attempted by Paul Williams, Decair Right, and Chris Humpreger. - Types: - Alpha Diversity: - Analytical study of biodiversity in various regions. - Beta Diversity: - Comparison of biodiversity between ecosystems. - Gamma Diversity: - Rate of species change with expanding geographical regions. - Importance of Biodiversity: - Supports ecosystem services essential for well-being. - Genetic diversity enables breeding of high-yield, disease-resistant plants/animals. - Ecosystems regulate climate, filter water/air, conserve soil/nutrients, control pests. - Provides food, building materials, energy, medicines. - Offers cultural services (recreational, aesthetic, spiritual values). - Factors Affecting Biodiversity: - Globalization: - Changes in food and agricultural commodity production. - Demand for high-value crops (e.g., soybeans, coffee, oil palm) leads to habitat conversion and ecosystem degradation. - Replaces diverse small farms with monoculture enterprises. - Inappropriate agricultural policies drive species and ecosystem service loss. - Population growth and consumption increase demand for ecosystem services and energy. - Effects of Biodiversity Loss: - Decline in plant production. - Reduced resistance and resilience to environmental changes (e.g., drought). - Increased variability in ecosystem processes (plant productivity, water use, disease). - Conservation of Biodiversity: - Purpose: - Restoring, protecting, or enhancing species and community diversity to maintain distribution. - Methods: - In-situ Conservation: - Conserves plants/animals in their native or man-made ecosystems. - Applies to wild fauna/flora, not domesticated species. - Examples: - National Parks, Sanctuaries, Biosphere Reserves, Cultural Landscapes, Gene Sanctuaries, Sacred Groves. - Ex-situ Conservation: - Conserves endangered species outside their natural habitats. - Examples: - Gene banks, seed banks, botanical gardens, tissue culture, cultural collections, zoos. - Biodiversity Hotspots: - Definition: - Areas with exceptional concentrations of endemic species and significant habitat loss. - Endemic species are unique to a specific region and vulnerable to extinction. - Proposed by Norman Myers in 1988. - Total: - 36 hotspots globally. - India’s Biodiversity Hotspots: - Himalaya: - Original extent: - 741,706 km². - Includes parts of Pakistan, Tibet, Nepal, Bhutan, China, Myanmar, India. - Transition zone between Palearctic and Indo-Malayan realms. - ~10,000 plant species, 3,160 endemic. - Home to vultures, tigers, elephants, rhinos, wild water buffalo. - Western Ghats and Sri Lanka: - Original extent: - 189,611 km². - Includes India and Sri Lanka. - ~9,916 plant species, 3,049 endemic (51.5%). - High endemism in reptiles, amphibians, freshwater fish. - ~25% of original vegetation remains pristine due to timber and agricultural demands. - Indo-Burma: - Extent: - 2,373,000 km², east of Ganges-Brahmaputra lowlands. - ~13,500 vascular plant species, ~7,000 endemic (52%). - 25 endemic threatened mammals, 35 endemic threatened amphibians. - ~5% of natural habitats remain pristine due to resource exploitation and habitat loss. - Sundaland: - Original extent: - 1,501,063 km². - Includes Thailand, Malaysia, Singapore, Brunei, Indonesia, Nicobar Islands (India). - ~25,000 vascular plant species, 15,000 endemic (60%). - Declining orangutan populations. - Threatened by industrial forestry and international animal trade. - Wetlands - Definition: - Land areas saturated with water (permanently or seasonally), characterized by hydric soil and aquatic plant vegetation. - Roles: - Water purification, flood control, shoreline stability. - Most biologically diverse ecosystems, supporting diverse plant and animal life. - Ramsar Convention: - International treaty signed in Ramsar, Iran, on February 2, 1971, effective December 21, 1975. - Aims for conservation and wise use of wetlands. - 171 contracting parties, 2,331 Ramsar sites globally. - Common Ramsar Wetlands in India: -
Wetland State
Nalsarovar Gujarat
Renuka Wetland Himachal Pradesh
Chandertal Wetland Himachal Pradesh
Hokera Wetland Jammu and Kashmir
Surinsar-Mansar Lake Jammu and Kashmir
Bhoj Wetland Madhya Pradesh
Chilika Odisha
Ropar Punjab
Sambar Lake Rajasthan
Upper Ganga River (Brijghat to Narora Stretch) Uttar Pradesh
- Mangroves: - Description: - Forest ecosystems with vegetation tolerant of high water quality and regular flooding. - Functions: - Prevent soil erosion, aid in land reclamation from seas. - Used for house building, furniture, transmission/telephone poles, household items. - National and International Conventions on Wildlife - CITES (Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species): - Signed by India on July 20, 1976. - Prohibits commercial trade of endangered species. - The Tiger Summit (November 2010): - Global Tiger Recovery Programme to double wild tiger populations by 2022. - Coalition Against Wildlife Trafficking (CAWT): - Global coalition of governments, businesses, and conservation organizations. - India joined with the US and others; first ministerial meeting in Nairobi, February 2007. - World Wide Fund for Nature (WWF): - International NGO focused on conservation, research, and restoration. - Over 5 million supporters, active in 100+ countries, supporting ~1,300 projects. - Mascot: - Giant Panda (Chi-chi, from Beijing to London Zoo). - TRAFFIC (Trade Records Analysis of Fauna and Flora in Commerce): - Monitors trade in endangered species, coordinates with CITES and IUCN’s Species Survival Commission to manage trade sustainably. - UNESCO World Heritage Convention: - Lists cultural and natural world heritage sites. - India’s Wildlife Department conserves sites like Kaziranga, Manas, Nanda Devi, and Keoladeo National Parks. - CMS (Convention on Migratory Species, Bonn Convention): - India signed an MoU in Bangkok (February 2007) for conservation of marine turtles in the Indian Ocean and Southeast Asia. - Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD): - Signed at the Earth Summit, Rio de Janeiro, June 5, 1992; effective December 29, 1993. - Goals: - Conservation of biodiversity. - Sustainable use of components. - Equitable sharing of genetic resource benefits. - Botanic Garden of India Republic (BGIR): - Established April 2002 under the Botanical Survey of India. - Part of the National Jai Vigyan Science and Technology Mission. - Cartagena Protocol on Biosafety: - Adopted January 29, 2000, as a supplementary agreement to the CBD. - IUCN Red List: - Founded in 1963, the most comprehensive inventory of species conservation status. - Classifications: - Extinct: - No individuals remaining. - Extinct in the Wild (EW): - Survives only in captivity. - Critically Endangered (CR): - Extremely high extinction risk. - Endangered (EN): - High extinction risk. - Vulnerable (VU): - High risk of endangerment. - Near Threatened (NT): - Likely to become endangered. - Least Concern (LC): - Lowest risk. - Data Deficient (DD): - Insufficient data for assessment. - Not Evaluated (NE): - Not assessed. - India’s Status: - 64 critically endangered animal species. - 132 plant and animal species listed as critically endangered (Red Data Book, February 18, 2012, Rio+20 Summit). - Agriculture Science - Definition: - A multidisciplinary field encompassing research and development focused on enhancing agricultural production techniques, improving productivity in terms of quantity and quality, and minimizing the impact of pests on crop and animal production systems. - Key Areas: - Production Techniques: - Includes irrigation management, recommended nitrogen inputs, etc. - Productivity Improvement: - Involves selection of drought-resistant crops and animals, development of new pesticides, yield-sensing technologies, etc. - Pest Management: - Addresses control of weeds, insects, pathogens, and nematodes affecting crops or animal systems. - Green Revolution: - A term coined in 1968 to describe the significant increase in food grain production in India. - Components: - High-yield variety (HYV) seeds. - Improved irrigation systems. - Use of fertilizers, insecticides, and pesticides. - Advanced scientific techniques such as mixed farming, intercropping, and integrated farming practices to boost food production. - Crop: - Definition: - Plants grown and cultivated on a large scale for commercial purposes. - Classification of Crops Based on Season: - Kharif Crops: - Grown during the rainy season (June to September/October-November). - Require high temperature and humid conditions. - Examples: - Maize, bajra, red gram, paddy, soybean, groundnut, cotton, millets, sorghum, moong bean, sugarcane, tea, coffee. - Also known as summer or monsoon crops. - Rabi Crops: - Grown during the winter season (October to March/April). - Require low temperatures during sowing and warmer, dry weather during maturity. - Depend on water percolated into the ground during the monsoon. - Examples: - Wheat, barley, mustard, pea, sesame, green gram, linseed. - Zaid Crops: - Grown during the summer season (March to June). - Tolerant to high light intensity and wind. - Examples: - Muskmelon, watermelon, cucumber, gourd. - Related Revolutions in Agriculture: - Blue Revolution: - Pertains to aquaculture, encompassing all aquatic animals and plants occurring in freshwater. - Golden Revolution: - Relates to horticulture, focusing on the production of fruits, vegetables, and other horticultural crops. - Improvement in Crop Yields: - Major Activities: - Grouped into three categories: crop variety improvement, crop production improvement, and crop protection management. - Crop Variety Improvement: - Aims to enhance crop yield and quality through various strategies. - Key Aspects: - Disease Resistance: - Protects crops from bacterial, viral, fungal diseases, etc. - Product Quality: - Enhances nutritional and processing quality, e.g., baking quality in wheat, protein quality in pulses, oil quality in oilseeds, and preserving quality in fruits and vegetables. - Biotic and Abiotic Resistance: - Increases resistance to diseases, insects, nematodes, and environmental stresses like drought, salinity, waterlogging, heat, cold, and frost. - Change in Maturity Duration: - Reduces time from sowing to harvesting, enabling multiple crop cycles and reducing production time. - Wider Adaptability: - Enhances crop stability across diverse environmental conditions. - Desirable Agronomic Characteristics: - Promotes traits like tallness and profuse branching in fodder crops or dwarfness in cereals for reduced nutrient consumption and higher productivity. - Methods for Improvement: - Hybridisation: - Incorporates desirable traits by crossing genetically dissimilar plants. - Types of crossing: - Intervarietal: - Between different varieties of the same species. - Interspecific: - Between two different species of the same genus. - Intergeneric: - Between different genera. - Genetic Modification: - Introduces specific genes to improve crop characteristics, e.g., golden rice with enhanced vitamin A content. - Crop Production Management: - Depends on investment in land, money, and technologies, linking inputs to yield. - Levels of Production: - No-cost production, low-cost production, high-cost production. - Key Aspects: - Nutrient Management: - Plants require 17 essential nutrients for growth, reproduction, and disease resistance. - Nutrient deficiency impacts physiological processes. - Nutrient Sources: - Manure: - Organic matter that enhances soil fertility and adds humus. - Types: - Compost and Vermi-Compost: - Derived from decomposed livestock excreta, vegetable waste, animal waste, sewage, and domestic waste through composting. - Green Manure: - Cover crops grown to add nutrients and organic matter to soil, plowed under while green or shortly after flowering. - Examples: - Legumes (cowpeas, soybeans, annual sweet clover, sunn hemp, sesbania, velvet beans) for nitrogen fixation; non-legumes (sudangrass, millet, sorghum, buckwheat) for weed suppression and biomass addition. - Uses of Manure: - Improves soil structure, enhancing nutrient and water retention. - Increases microbial activity, boosting trace mineral supply. - Enhances water-holding capacity in sandy soils and prevents waterlogging in clayey soils. - Chemical Fertilizers: - Organic or inorganic materials supplying essential plant nutrients. - Provide six macronutrients: nitrogen (N), phosphorus (P), potassium (K), calcium (Ca), magnesium (Mg), sulfur (S). - Provide eight micronutrients: boron (B), chlorine (Cl), copper (Cu), iron (Fe), manganese (Mn), molybdenum (Mo), zinc (Zn), nickel (Ni). - Uses: - Promote vegetative growth (leaves, branches, flowers) for healthy plants. - Contribute to higher yields in high-cost farming. - Replace nutrients lost in potted plants. - Biofertilizers: - Microbial products or microorganisms that enrich soil nutrients. - Nitrogen-Fixing Bacteria: - Convert atmospheric nitrogen into plant-usable inorganic compounds. - Types: - Free-Living (Non-Symbiotic): - Cyanobacteria (Anabaena, Nostoc), Azotobacter, Beijerinckia, Clostridium. - Mutualistic (Symbiotic): - Rhizobium (with legumes), Spirillum, Lipoferum (with cereal grasses). - Symbiotic Process: - Bacteria invade root hairs, multiply, and form root nodules, converting nitrogen to ammonia for plant use. - Seeds of legumes (e.g., alfalfa, beans, clovers, peas, soybeans) are inoculated with Rhizobium cultures for optimal nodule formation in poor soils. - Irrigation: - Artificial application of controlled water amounts to agricultural lands using wells (dug wells, tube wells), canals, river lift systems, and tanks. - Initiatives to Increase Water Availability: - Rainwater harvesting. - Watershed management. - Building small check-dams to raise groundwater levels and prevent soil erosion. - Cropping Patterns: - Managed through agronomy, the science of field crop production and soil management. - Definition: - Sequence of crops grown on a piece of land over a fixed period, interacting with farm resources. - Types: - Crop Rotation: - Growing dissimilar crops sequentially in the same area to improve soil structure and fertility. - Alternates deep-rooted and shallow-rooted plants. - Replenishes nitrogen using green manure in sequence with cereals. - Examples: - One-year rotation: - Paddy and wheat. - Two-year rotation: - Maize and cotton. - Three-year rotation: - Tomato and lady’s finger. - Four-year rotation: - Cotton and wheat. - Mixed Cropping: - Growing two or more crops together in the same area during a single season. - Combinations: - Tall crops with short crops, e.g., maize with urad. - Legumes with non-legumes, e.g., arhar with jowar. - Tap-rooted crops with shallow-rooted crops. - Erect crops with bushy crops. - Short-duration crops with long-duration crops. - Methods: - Mixed Crops: - Seeds of different crops mixed and sown together in lines or by broadcasting. - Guard Crops: - Main crop surrounded by thorn crops, e.g., safflower around pea crops. - Companion Crops: - Different crops sown without mixing seeds, e.g., 2-3 rows of mustard with 5-8 rows of wheat. - Augmented Crops: - Sub-crops grown to supplement the main crop’s yield. - Intensive Cropping: - Characterized by low fallow ratios and high inputs (capital, labor, pesticides, fertilizers). - Associated with increased agricultural mechanization. - Intercropping: - Growing two or more crops in close proximity to maximize cooperation and minimize competition. - Aims to utilize resources efficiently for higher yields. - Terrace Farming: - Farming on sloped land landscaped into terraces, common in North-Eastern hilly regions of India. - Crop Protection Management: - Protects crops from weeds, insect pests, and diseases. - Weeds: - Unwanted plants competing for food, space, and light, reducing crop growth. - Examples: - Xanthium (gokhroo), Parthenium (gajar ghas), Cyperinus rotundus (motha). - Control Methods: - Mechanical removal. - Proper seedbed preparation. - Timely sowing. - Intercropping and crop rotation. - Insect Pests: - Damage plants by: - Cutting roots, stems, and leaves. - Sucking cell sap. - Invading stems and fruits. - Control Methods: - Use of pesticides, insecticides, herbicides, and fungicides. - Excessive use may cause health and environmental issues. - Integrated Pest Management (IPM): - A comprehensive approach integrating pesticides/herbicides with other practices for economic pest control. - Components: - Acceptable pest levels (action thresholds). - Preventive cultural practices. - Monitoring. - Mechanical controls. - Biological controls. - Responsible pesticide use. - Storage of Grains: - Protection extends to grain storage to prevent losses from biotic factors (insects, fungi, mites, rodents, bacteria) and abiotic factors (inappropriate moisture, storage temperature). - Effects: - Reduced quality, weight, germinability, and coloration of grains. - Preventive Measures: - Cleaning. - Proper drying. - Fumigation with pest-killing chemicals. - Types of Crops and Their Origins: - Cereal Crops (Gramineae Family): -

- Area and Production of Important Crops in India: - Wheat: - Leading State: - Uttar Pradesh (highest area and production). - Punjab: - Higher production per hectare. - Rice: - Leading State: West Bengal (highest area and production). - Maize: - Leading State: Uttar Pradesh (highest area and production). - Jowar: - Leading State: Maharashtra (highest area and production). - Total Pulses: - Leading State: Madhya Pradesh (highest area and production). - Total Oilseeds: - Leading State: Madhya Pradesh (highest area and production). - Arhar: - Leading State: Maharashtra (highest area and production). - Gram: - Leading State: Madhya Pradesh (highest area and production). - Rapeseed and Mustard: - Leading State: Rajasthan (highest area and production). - Cotton: - Leading State in Area: Maharashtra. - Leading State in Production: Punjab. - Tobacco: - Leading State: Gujarat. - Other Fields for Improvement in Agriculture: - Seed Science: - Focuses on seed quality, ensuring seeds are free from adulterants, diseases, or pest infestations. - Types of Seeds: - Synthetic and Composite Varieties: - Synthetic: - Developed by selecting inbred lines with good General Combining Ability (GCA). - Composite: - Developed by mixing seeds of outstanding inbred lines with similar characteristics. - Examples: - Sona (Rice), Shakti (Tall), African (Maize). - Hybrid Seeds: - Produced by cross-pollination, a major contributor to increased agricultural output. - Artificial Seeds: - Encapsulated plant propagules (somatic embryos/shoot buds) in a matrix containing nutrients, growth regulators, herbicides, insecticides, or fungicides. - Genetically Modified Seeds: - Altered through biotechnology to enhance quality, e.g., improved resistance or nutritional content. - Integrated Scheme of Oilseeds, Pulses, Oil Palm, and Maize (ISOPOM): - Implemented in 2004 to focus on improving production of oilseeds, pulses, oil palm, and maize. - Agroforestry: - Combines trees and shrubs with crops and/or livestock for a diverse, profitable, healthy, and sustainable land-use system. - Objectives: - Optimize farm resource utilization. - Maximize food, fodder, and fuel production. - Enhance biological and physiological resource use. - Blanching: - A method of covering young plant shoots to exclude light, preventing chlorophyll production and keeping shoots pale for delicate flavor and texture. - Commonly Blanched Vegetables: - Cardoon. - Celery. - Chicory (Cichorium intybus). - Good King Henry (Chenopodium bonus-henricus). - Leek. - Sea kale (Crambe maritima). - Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR): - Established: July 16, 1929 (formerly Imperial Council of Agricultural Research). - Role: - Apex body for coordinating, guiding, and managing research and education in agriculture, horticulture, fisheries, and animal sciences. - Infrastructure: 97 operational institutes and 53 agricultural universities in India. - Achievements: - Received the King Baudouin Development Prize in 1988 and 2004 for the Rice-Wheat Consortium. - Key Institutes: - Indian Agricultural Research Institute, New Delhi. - National Dairy Research Institute, Karnal. - Central Institute for Research on Cattle, Meerut. - Central Institute on Fisheries Education, Mumbai. - India’s Position in World Agriculture: - 1st: - Buffalo, tea, jute, mango, coconut, cauliflower, spices, banana, milk production, irrigation, oilseeds, hybrid cotton. - 2nd: - Groundnut, wheat, irrigated land, paddy, fruits, onion, cow, goat, vegetables, sugarcane, tobacco. - 3rd: - Apple. - 4th: - Coarse grains, tractors, fertilizer use. - 5th: - Egg production, natural rubber, coffee production. - 6th: - Soybean production. - International Observances Related to Agriculture: - 2004: - International Rice Year. - 2005: - Neem Patent Year. - 2006: - Parthenium Control and Awareness Year. - 2007: - International Water Year. - 2008: - World Potato Year. - 2010: - International Year of Biodiversity. - 2011: - International Year for Forests. - 2012: - International Year of Sustainable Energy for All. - 2013: - International Year of Quinoa. - 2014: - International Year of Family Farming. - 2015: - International Year of Soil. - 2016: - International Year of Pulses (IYP). - Economic Zoology - Definition: - A branch of biology focusing on animals that are economically significant for human use, such as producing eggs, honey, meat, and other products, as well as controlling or eradicating harmful animals. - Includes: - Culturing animals for mass production of useful products. - Managing or eliminating animals that are directly or indirectly harmful to humans. - Animal Husbandry - Definition: - The science of breeding, managing, and caring for farm animals to benefit humans. - A branch of agriculture concerned with the care and breeding of domestic animals like cattle, pigs, sheep, and horses. - Promotes desirable traits in animals for utility, pleasure, and research. - Breeding: - Involves crossing animals of two breeds (groups related by descent with similar characteristics) to enhance desirable traits. - Pioneered as a scientific practice by Robert Bakewell through selective breeding. - Types of Breeding Programmes: - Inbreeding: - Mating of closely related individuals for 4–6 generations. - Increases homozygosity, stabilizing pure lines but reducing genetic variation. - Continued inbreeding may lead to inbreeding depression, causing loss of productivity. - Outbreeding: - Mating of unrelated individuals to increase genetic diversity. - Subcategories: - Outcrossing: - Mating between unrelated members of the same breed with no common ancestors in 4–6 generations. - Resultant individual is called an outcross. - Cross Breeding: - Crossing a superior male of one breed with a superior female of another breed. - Example: - Bikaneri ewes × Merino rams produce Hissardale sheep. - Interspecific Hybridisation: - Crossing male and female animals of two different species. - Examples: - Mule (horse × donkey), hinny (donkey × horse). - Other Breeding Methods: - Artificial Insemination: - Semen is collected from a male and manually introduced into the female’s oviduct for fertilization. - Commonly used in sheep, horses, cattle, pigs, dogs, pedigree animals, and honeybees. - Economical method to improve herds with desirable traits. - Semen can be preserved (e.g., frozen bovine serum) for future use, even after the donor’s death. - Embryo Transfer: - An assisted reproductive technique where embryos are placed into a female’s uterus to establish pregnancy. - Can involve transplanting embryos from high-quality females to surrogate mothers after laboratory fertilization. - Requires successful embryo implantation in the uterus. - Improves feed-to-product conversion (meat, milk, fiber) and product quality. - May reduce genetic diversity and increase disease outbreak severity. - Rearing Animals - Definition: - A component of animal husbandry involving raising animals, practiced since the transition from hunter-gatherer to farming lifestyles. - Cattle Farming: - Involves rearing cattle for milk production and draught labor (e.g., irrigation, tilling, carting). - Criteria for Good Cattle Farming: - Hygienic Conditions: - Proper cleaning, sanitation, and body care (e.g., dirt removal by brushing and loosening hair). - Management and Shelter Facilities: - Safe, well-ventilated shelters to protect from rain, heat, and cold. - Nutrition: - Adequate water and food, including roughage (fiber) and concentrates (low-fiber, high-protein, nutrient-rich feeds) for milk quality and cattle health. - Disease Control: - Vaccination to immunize cattle against infections. - Cow: - Scientific Names: - Bos indicus, Bos taurus. - Population: - India accounts for ~20% of the world’s cattle population, with ~30 varieties. - Breeds: - Milch Breeds: - Specialized for milk production. - Examples: - Gir: - Originates from Gir hills, Gujarat; tolerates stress; yields ~1746 L milk/lactation. - Red Sindhi: - Originates from Karachi, Pakistan; red body with white patches; yields ~5440 kg milk/lactation. - Sahiwal: - From Sahiwal district, Pakistan; yields ~2725–4735 kg milk/lactation with 4–6% fat. - Deoni: - From Maharashtra and Karnataka, derived from Gir ~500 years ago. - Draught Breeds: - Used for labor in fields. - Examples: - Malvi: - Found in Malwa, Madhya Pradesh, and Jhalawar, Rajasthan; dark spots on neck, dished forehead. - Hallikar: - Southern India; medium-sized, muscular, long face, bulgy forehead. - General Utility (Dual Purpose) Breeds: - Good for both milk production and draught work. - Examples: - Short-horned, white/light grey cattle with long, coffin-shaped skulls, slightly convex faces (e.g., Hariana, Ongole, Gaolo, Rath, Dangi, Krishna Valley, Himgiri). - Long-horned, grey, deep-bodied cattle with wide foreheads, good draught capacity (e.g., Tharparkar, Kankrej). - Nagori: - White/light grey, from Nagaur, Rajasthan. - Kangayam: - From Tamil Nadu; dark-colored hump, back, and forehead. - Exotic Breeds: - Non-native breeds for milk production. - Examples: - Jersey: - Almond-colored, spotted; yields ~4500 L milk/year with 5% fat. - Gersy: - Yields ~45–50 L milk/day with 4% fat. - Brown Swiss: - Yields ~5200 L milk/year with 4% fat. - Milk Production Factors: - Depends on lactation period (time of milk production post-calf birth). - Longer lactation periods increase milk yield. - Buffaloes: - Scientific Name: Bubalus bubalis. - Origin: - Likely India. - Types: - Exotic or Marshy Breeds: - Found in Myanmar, Philippines, Malaysia, Thailand, Singapore, Indonesia, China. - Used for carting (e.g., Jarangi, Khuzestani, Ongole, Sinhala, Manoti). - Aquatic or Indian Breeds: - Prefer water, low heat tolerance. - Subcategories: - Light Weight: - Bhadawari: - Found in Uttar Pradesh and Madhya Pradesh; yields ~800–1000 kg milk/lactation. - Nagpuri (Barari): - Found in Maharashtra; white patches on face; yields ~700–1200 kg milk/lactation. - Surti (Daccani Gujarati): - Rusty brown to silver-grey coat; yields ~1000–1300 kg milk/lactation. - Heavy Weight: - Mehsana: - Dairy breed from Maharashtra and Gujarat; cross of Surti and Murrah; yields ~8–9 L milk/day. - Jaffrabadi: - Heaviest Indian breed, from Gujarat; yields ~1000–1200 kg milk/lactation. - Murrah: - From Haryana; jet black; yields ~1500–2500 kg milk/lactation. - Nili Ravi: - From Ravi River area; has wall eyes; yields ~1500–1850 kg milk/lactation. - Poultry Farming: - Involves raising domestic birds (e.g., chickens, geese, fowl) for eggs and meat (broilers). - Chickens are the most numerous. - Criteria for Good Poultry Farming: - Good Growth Rate: - Vitamin-rich supplementary feed for broilers enhances growth. - Mortality Rate: - Proper care reduces mortality and maintains feathering and carcass quality. - Management: - Maintain temperature, hygiene (cleaning, sanitation), disease control (disinfectant spraying), and quality feed. - Nutritional Requirements: - Daily feed includes protein-rich, vitamin A and K-rich, and adequate fat content for broilers. - Immunization: - Required vaccinations for poultry health. - Fowls: - Layers: - Raised for eggs. - Broilers: - Raised for meat. - Desired Traits: - High number and quality of chicks. - Dwarf broiler parents for commercial chick production. - Adaptability to different temperatures. - Low maintenance requirements. - Smaller egg-laying birds that utilize fibrous, cheaper diets from agricultural by-products. - Chicken Breeds: - Basra: - From Maharashtra and Gujarat; used for meat. - Aseel: - From South Punjab/Sindh, Pakistan/India; used for meat. - Ghagas: - From Andhra Pradesh and Mysore; used for meat. - Chittagong: - From Bangladesh; used for eggs and meat. - Langshan (Exotic): - Rare, from China; used for egg production. - Sheep and Goats: - Sheep: - Reared for wool, skin, and mutton. - Lifespan: - ~13 years. - Indian Breeds: -

- Exotic Breeds: - Saanen, Angora, Matau, Kambing Katjang, Khursani, Damascus, Marwari, Mubende, Toggenburg, Baluchi, Nubian, Bodur, Sudan Nubian. - Pigs (Swine or Hog): - Scientific Names: - Descended from Sus scrofa (European wild boar) or cross with Sus indicus (Asiatic). - Piggery: - Care and management of pigs, significant in North-Eastern India. - Importance: - Efficient food converters, improve socio-economic status of weaker sections. - Breeds: - Exotic: - Landrace, Large White Yorkshire, Hampshire, Berkshire, Saddleback. - Domesticated: - Ghori, Desi. - Health: - Susceptible to swine flu. - Camels: - Known as the "ship of the desert," used as beasts of burden in hot desert and semi-desert regions. - Survive on minimal food and water while carrying loads. - Types: - Arabian Camel: - Camelus dromedarius. - Turkish or Bactrian Camel: - Camelus bactrianus. - Major Breed Improvement Programmes: - Intensive Cattle Development Programme: - Established by Gujarat Government in 1965–66. - Objective: - Preserve and improve genetic characteristics of breeds. - Gaushala Development Programme: - Focuses on improving indigenous cattle through selective breeding and artificial insemination with quality semen in Gaushalas. - Central Rural Yojana: - Started in 1952 to develop dual-purpose cattle. - Trial Reproduction Programme: - Aims to improve cattle in high-altitude regions. - Animal Diseases: - Caused by fungi, bacteria, viruses, and parasites; specific to animals, not typically affecting humans. - Fungal Diseases: -
